Undecided State Dynamics with Many Opinions
Colin Cooper, Frederik Mallmann-Trenn, Tomasz Radzik, Nobutaka Shimizu, Takeharu Shiraga

Abstract
We study the Undecided-State Dynamics (USD), a fundamental consensus process in which each vertex holds one of k k decided opinions or the undecided state. We consider both the gossip model and the population protocol model. Prior work established tight bounds on the consensus time of this process only for the regime k = O ⁡ ( n / ( log ⁡ n ) 2 ) k=O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2}) (for the population protocol model) and k = O ⁡ ( ( n / log ⁡ n ) 1 / 3 ) k=O((n/\log n)^{1/3}) (for the gossip model), often under restrictive assumptions on the initial configuration. In this paper, we obtain the first consensus-time guarantees for USD that hold for arbitrary 2 ≤ k ≤ n 2\leq k\leq n and for arbitrary initial configurations in both the gossip model and the population protocol model. In the gossip model, USD reaches consensus within O ~ ​ ( min ⁡ { k , n } ) \widetilde{O}(\min\{k,\sqrt{n}\}) synchronous rounds with probability 1 − p ⊥ − n − c 1-p_{\bot}-n^{-c} , where p ⊥ p_{\bot} is the gossip-specific probability of collapsing to the all-undecided state in the first round. In the population protocol model, USD reaches consensus within O ~ ​ ( min ⁡ { k ​ n , n 3 / 2 } ) \widetilde{O}(\min\{kn,n^{3/2}\}) asynchronous interactions with high probability. We also present lower bounds that match the upper bounds up to polylogarithmic factors for a specific initial configuration and show that our upper bounds are essentially optimal.



1
Introduction

The Undecided-State Dynamics (USD)
[
3
,
5
]

is one of the simplest and most studied consensus dynamics on complete graphs.
Each vertex holds an opinion in a finite set
\Sigma=[k]\cup\{\bot\}
,
where
[k]:=\{1,\dots,k\}
are decided opinions and
\bot
is a distinguished
undecided opinion.
When a vertex
u
interacts with a vertex
v
,
the update follows a simple cancellation/adoption rule (with only
u
updating its opinion):
if
u
and
v
hold different decided opinions in
[k]
, then
u
becomes
\bot
;
if
u
is undecided and
v
is decided, then
u
adopts
v
’s opinion;
otherwise
u
keeps its opinion.
This elementary rule (formalized in
Section

3.1
)
already gives rise to rich and nontrivial behavior.

We analyze USD with
k
possible decided opinions on an
n
-vertex complete
graph with self-loops
1
1

1

Thus, choosing a random neighbor is equivalent to choosing a vertex uniformly at random.
under two classical communication models.
In the
gossip model

[
29
]
, time proceeds in synchronous rounds
in which each vertex samples one random neighbor and simultaneously updates its opinion accordingly.
In the
population protocol model

[
2
]
, interactions occur
asynchronously: an ordered pair
(u,v)
is chosen uniformly at random with replacement, and

u
updates its opinion based on
v
’s opinion.

A central quantity of interest is the
consensus time
: the number of
rounds (in the gossip model) or pairwise interactions (in the population protocol model)
until all vertices hold the same decided opinion in

[k]
. The all-
\bot
configuration, although
absorbing, does not constitute consensus and is regarded as failure. Consensus
time therefore measures the time to reach agreement on a real opinion.
In what follows, we review previous consensus time bounds on USD. For results on other relevant consensus dynamics, see
Section

1.2
.

USD was first introduced by
[
3
]
for
k=2
in the
population protocol model, where they showed that the consensus time is

O(n\log n)
with high probability.
2
2

2

A given event holds “with high probability,” if it holds with probability
1-O(n^{-c})
for some constant
c>0
.

They (and
[
13
]
) further proved that USD with
k=2
reaches consensus with the
initial majority with high probability if the more popular opinion has
a sufficiently large initial advantage compared
to the other opinions.
Thus USD offered a critical performance improvement over the classic pull voting, both in terms of the consensus time (expected
\Theta(n^{2})
in pull voting
[
27
,
15
]
) and the chance
for the majority opinion to win.
For larger
k
,
[
1
]
analyzed USD
for
2\leq k=O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2})
and proved that the consensus time is

O(kn\log n)
with high probability for any initial configuration.
Recently,
[
21
]
obtained a matching lower bound of

\Omega(kn\log n)
for all
k\leq n^{1/2-\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon
is an arbitrary
positive constant.

In the gossip model, USD was first analyzed by
[
5
]
, who
proved an
O(k\log n)
upper bound for
2\leq k=O((n/\log n)^{1/3})

under a strong
assumption on the initial advantage of the most popular opinion. This
assumption was later removed for
k=2
by
[
12
]
, who showed an

O(\log n)
bound for arbitrary initial configurations.
However, no general bound was known for any
k>2
without any assumptions
on the initial configuration.

It is natural to conjecture that the consensus time in the gossip model should be

\widetilde{O}(k)
3
3

3

Throughout the paper,
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
hides polylogarithmic factors in
n
.
for
2\leq k\ll\sqrt{n}
,
4
4

4

“
k\ll f(n)
” abbreviates that
k=O(f(n)/\log^{c}n)
for some constant
c>0
.

by comparison with the

\widetilde{O}(kn)
upper bound of
[
1
]
for the
population protocol model: a single synchronous round of the gossip model is
roughly equivalent to
n
asynchronous interactions in the population protocol
model. However, the analysis of
[
1
]
critically
exploits the asynchronous nature of population protocols and does not transfer
to the gossip model. Indeed, they explicitly identify the gossip-model bound as
an open problem
[
1
, Section 9]
.

Moreover, prior work provides a rather clear picture only in the regime

k\ll\sqrt{n}
: In the population protocol model, the best-known upper
and lower bounds are
\widetilde{\Theta}(kn)
in this range

[
1
,
21
]
, strongly suggesting that
the consensus time scales linearly with
k
.
In the gossip model, the only existing upper bounds for USD with
k>2

are also of order
\widetilde{O}(k)
, but they hold only under strong
assumptions on the initial configuration and only for
k\ll n^{1/3}

[
5
]
.
Beyond this threshold, however, the efficiency of USD is mysterious:
the analytical techniques used before do not work for larger values of
k

and no general consensus-time bounds are available for any
k\geq\sqrt{n}
in
the population protocol model or any
k\geq{n}^{1/3}
in the gossip model.
This raises a natural and central question:
does the linear-in-
k
behavior of the consensus time persist for all
k
,
or does USD transition to a qualitatively different—possibly sublinear—regime
once
k
exceeds
\sqrt{n}
?
Addressing this question requires techniques that go well beyond those used
in the
k<\sqrt{n}
regime, and lies at the core of our contribution.

1.1
Our Contributions

We obtain the first general bounds on the consensus time of the
Undecided-State Dynamics (USD) for an
arbitrary

number
k
of decided opinions and an
arbitrary
initial configuration in both the gossip model and the
population protocol model. Our main results are summarized in the following
informal theorem. See also
Table

1
.

Theorem 1.1

(Main Theorem)
.

Consider USD on
n
vertices starting from an arbitrary initial configuration
5
5

5

A configuration is an assignment of opinions to vertices.

in
\{1,\dots,k,\bot\}^{n}

other than the all-
\bot
configuration, where the number of
possible decided opinions
k
is an arbitrary integer between
2
and
n
(inclusive).

•

Gossip model.

In the gossip model, USD reaches consensus within

\widetilde{O}\left\lparen\min\{k,\sqrt{n}\}\right\rparen
synchronous rounds with probability

1-p_{\bot}-n^{-c}
for some constant
c>0
, where
p_{\bot}
is the probability that all vertices hold
\bot
after the first synchronous round.

•

Population protocol model.

In the population protocol model, USD reaches consensus within

\widetilde{O}\left\lparen\min\{kn,n^{1.5}\}\right\rparen
asynchronous interactions
with high probability.

Our result for the gossip model says that, unless the process collapses to the
all-
\bot
configuration in the first round, it reaches consensus within

\widetilde{O}\left\lparen\min\{k,\sqrt{n}\}\right\rparen
rounds with high probability.

Regarding the hidden
\mathrm{polylog}(n)
factor in the consensus time, we have the following bounds:
In the gossip model, the consensus time is, with high probability,
O(\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3})
for arbitrary
2\leq k\leq n
, and
O(k\log n)
if
k=O\quantity(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{(\log n)^2})
(the similar bound holds for the population protocol model).
Our results hold for every
2\leq k\leq n
and for arbitrary initial
configurations, including those with undecided vertices.

Comparison of consensus time bounds for USD in the Population Protocol (PP) and Gossip models.
Here
A_{i}
denotes the number of vertices holding opinion
i
in the initial configuration, with

A_{1}\geq A_{2}\geq\cdots\geq A_{k}
.
The assumptions on the initial gap are stated only when required; entries marked by “—”
indicate that no explicit gap assumption is imposed.

Remark 1.2
.

The probability
p_{\bot}
of collapsing to the all-
\bot
configuration in the
first synchronous round is a phenomenon specific to the gossip model.
Because all vertices update simultaneously, a single round can eliminate all
decided opinions. In contrast, in the population protocol model only one
vertex updates its opinion at each interaction, and an undecided vertex cannot
be created unless two distinct decided opinions are present.

The value of
p_{\bot}
strongly depends on the initial distribution of opinions.
For example, if
k=O(n/\log n)
, or if the initial configuration contains at
least one but not all undecided vertices, then
p_{\bot}=n^{-\Omega(1)}

(see
Lemma

5.1
).
At the opposite extreme, if
k=n
and each vertex initially holds a distinct
decided opinion,
then it becomes undecided in the first round with probability

1-1/n
, independently of the other vertices,
and thus
p_{\bot}=(1-1/n)^{n}\approx 1/e
.

Matching lower bounds.

We complement our upper bounds for the gossip model by proving a matching lower bound up to
a polylogarithmic factor in
n
.
Such a lower bound in the population protocol model is already given by the result of
[
21
]
in the regime of
k\leq n^{1/2-\varepsilon}
for any constant
\varepsilon>0
.

Theorem 1.3

(matching lower bound for the gossip model)
.

For any sufficiently large
n
, any constant
0<c<1/2
and any
2\leq k\leq(1/2-c)n
, there exists an initial configuration over exactly
k
decided opinions (i.e., each of the
k
opinions is supported by at least one vertex) such that USD in the gossip model requires

\widetilde{\Omega}\left\lparen\min\{k,\sqrt{n}\}\right\rparen
rounds to reach
consensus with high probability.

1.2
Other Related Works

Previous works on USD consider the plurality consensus problem and they show that USD solves it correctly with high probability if the initially most popular opinion has a significant advantage over the others.

[
11
,
26
]
presented protocols based on USD in the gossip model and showed that their protocols solve the plurality consensus problem correctly with high probability within
\widetilde{O}(1)
synchronous rounds with
\absolutevalue{\Sigma}=O(k)
states under the assumption that the most popular opinion has a margin of at least
\Omega(\sqrt{n\log n})
vertices over any other opinion in the initial configuration.
Recently,
[
4
]
presented protocols having similar flavor based on USD in both the population protocol model and the gossip model.
A remarkable feature of these protocols is that the consensus time is small for any
k\geq 2
and any initial configuration, whereas the protocols of
[
26
,
11
]
require the initial margin assumption to bound the consensus time.
There is a work on another variant of USD that deals with a preferred opinion
[
9
]
and under noisy channel
[
20
]
for
k=2
.

A long line of works studied the consensus times of 3-Majority and related dynamics (e.g., 2-Choices) with
k>2
opinions on an
n
-vertex complete graph
[
6
,
7
,
25
,
10
,
14
,
31
,
22
]
.
Recently,
[
14
]
showed that 3-Majority in the asynchronous update model (i.e., at every round, a uniformly random vertex is allowed to update its opinion) reaches consensus within
\widetilde{O}(\min\quantity{k,\sqrt{n}})
rounds.
In the synchronous update model where all vertices simultaneously update their opinions at every round,
[
31
]
showed that the consensus time of 2-Choices is
\widetilde{O}(k)
and that of 3-Majority is
\widetilde{O}(\min\quantity{k,\sqrt{n}})
.
Interestingly, this consensus time bound for 3-Majority is the same as the one for USD in the gossip model.

Several other relevant consensus dynamics have been considered in the literature.
A model that it somewhat similar is the model proposed in
[
23
]
, where initialy some nodes do not have an opinion, and adopt any opnion immedieatly. Afterwards, the process behaves exactly like the voter model.
The deterministic majority model, where each agent sets its opinion to the majroity among all of its neighbors, has been studied in
[
28
]
.
See
[
8
]
for more details on consensus dynamics.

2
Proof Overview

We outline the proof of
Theorem

1.1
for the gossip model and compare it with arguments from previous work.
For simplicity, we assume that
k\leq n/\log n
throughout this section.
This is only to ensure that with high probability USD does not fall into the all-
\bot
configuration within one round; it can be relaxed to
k\leq n
without much difficulty.

2.1
Process in a Nutshell

Following prior work on USD, we track the evolution of the number of
vertices holding each decided opinion.
For
t\geq 0
and
i\in[k]
, define

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}(i):=\text{fraction of vertices holding opinion $i$ at the beginning of round $t$}.

Then USD can be viewed as a Markov chain
(\alpha_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for
\alpha_{t}=(\alpha_{t}(1),\dots,\alpha_{t}(k))
on the state space

\{0,\frac{1}{n},\frac{2}{n},\ldots,\frac{n-1}{n},1\}^{k}
.
The key progress parameter is

\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}:=\max_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i),

and the consensus is reached at the first time
t
when
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}=1
.
Unless all vertices hold
\bot
, it holds that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq 1/n
.

We also introduce the following quantities:

\displaystyle\beta_{t}:=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i),\quad\psi_{t}:=\beta_{t}(2\beta_{t}-1)-\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)^{2}.

(1)

Note that
\beta_{t}
is the total fraction of vertices holding a decided opinion.
Roughly speaking,
\psi_{t}
is a key parameter that controls the stability of USD.
See
Section

2.3
for more details.

Call an opinion
i

weak at round
t
if
\alpha_{t}(i)\leq 0.9\cdot\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
strong at round
t
if
\alpha_{t}(i)\geq 0.95\cdot\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
.
Note that there is a margin between weak and strong opinions, which is crucial for our analysis.
We decompose the analysis into four parts.

Lemma 2.1

(informal; see
Lemmas

5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
,
5.6
,
5.16
and
5.20
for the formal statements)
.

Consider USD in the gossip model such that
k\leq n/\log n
and
\beta_{0}\geq 1/n
.

(I)

For some
T_{\beta}=O(\log n)
, with high probability,
for all
t\geq T_{\beta}
,
\beta_{t}\geq 1/2-o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
. Moreover, with high probability, for all
t\geq 1
,
\psi_{t}=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.

(II)

Suppose that
\psi_{0}=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
and
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
. For some
T_{\alpha}=O(\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3})
, with high probability,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq\Omega\quantity(\frac{(\log n)^2}{\sqrt{n}})
holds for all
t\geq T_{\alpha}
.

(III)

Suppose that
\psi_{0}=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\Omega\quantity(\frac{(\log n)^2}{\sqrt{n}})
. Then, for some
T^{\prime}=O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
, there exists exactly one strong opinion at round
T^{\prime}
.

(IV)

Suppose that
\psi_{0}=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\alpha_{0}(1)\geq\Omega\quantity(\frac{(\log n)^2}{\sqrt{n}})
, and
\alpha_{0}(1)\geq(1+c)\cdot\alpha_{0}(i)
for some constant
c>0
and all
i\geq 2
. Then, USD reaches consensus within
O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds with high probability.

Figure 1:
Combining the four parts of
Lemma

2.1
to prove the consensus time bound.

The four items above immediately imply that the consensus time is
O(\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3})
.
Moreover, (I) ensures that with high probability, for some
t=O(\log n)
,
\beta_{t}=\Omega(1)
,
implying that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}=\Omega(1/k)
.
Thus, if
k\leq\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2}
, then (III) and (IV) imply the consensus time bound of
O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})=O(k\log n)
.
Therefore, the consensus time is
\widetilde{O}(\min\quantity{k, \sqrt{n}})
(see
Fig.

1
).

Our main technical contribution is to establish part (II).
An important feature is that (II) holds regardless of how large
k
is, so
our analysis broadens the range of
k
from
k\ll n^{1/3}
(or
k\ll n^{1/2}
for the population protocol model) in the previous works to
k\leq n/\log n
.

Establishing part (III) is also challenging.

[
1
, Phases 1 to 3]
proved (III), for
k=O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2})
for the population protocol model, but their argument crucially relies on the local update property of the population protocol model which cannot be applied to the gossip model.

Part (IV) was shown in an earlier work
[
5
, Theorem 3.2]
for
k=O((n/\log n)^{1/3})
,
which (together with
\beta_{0}=\Omega(1)
) implies
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\frac{C\log^{1/3}n}{n^{1/3}}
, but we prove it under a weaker condition that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\Omega\quantity(\frac{(\log n)^2}{\sqrt{n}})
by combining the argument of
[
5
]
with sharp concentration inequalities from
[
14
,
31
]
.
In this section, we outline the proofs of (I) to (III) and omit the proof of (IV) for brevity.

2.2
Difficulties in Extending Previous Techniques

Before describing the ideas for the proof of
Lemma

2.1
,
we briefly discuss the techniques used in previous work and explain why they cannot be applied to USD directly.

(1) Breakdown of concentration-based analysis for large
k
.

The key observation introduced by
[
5
]
is that
\beta_{t}
concentrates around
1/2
and remains near this value for a
substantial period of time.
Using this stabilization property, they divided the USD process into several
phases according to
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
proved how the gap between the most popular opinion and the second most popular opinion grows in each phase.
This strategy is used in later work
[
1
]
to analyze USD in the population protocol model.
Our proof is based on this idea.

[
5
]
needed
in their analysis a strong assumption that
k\ll n^{1/3}
due to the limitation of the standard Chernoff bound.
In this paper, we broaden the range of
k
to
k\ll\sqrt{n}
by exploiting the wider martingale-based framework used in

[
14
,
31
]
—particularly, we use Freedman’s inequality
together with additional technical ideas (see
Section

2.6
for more details).

However, for
k\gg\sqrt{n}
, the tail bounds from Freedman’s inequality do not suffice to yield high-probability bounds, and the analysis based on the concentration of
\alpha_{t}(i)
and
\beta_{t}
as in
[
5
]
entirely breaks down.

Remark 2.2
.

In the gossip model, the standard concentration argument yields that the number of remaining decided opinions becomes with high probability at most
\min(k,n\log n/k)
after the first round if
\beta_{0}=1
(see
Appendix

C
), i.e., if all vertices are initially decided. On the other hand, if we could extend the argument in
[
1
]
from the population protocol model to the gossip model, we would be able to derive an
\widetilde{O}(k)
bound on the consensus time for
k\leq O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2})
.
Combining these two observations, we would be able to
obtain a
\widetilde{O}(\min\quantity{k, \sqrt{n}})
bound on the consensus time if either
k\leq O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2})
or
k\geq\Omega(\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3})
holds.
Thus even if it was possible to transfer somehow the analysis in

[
1
]
to the gossip model, the case of

O(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^{2})\leq k\leq\Omega(\sqrt{n}(\log n)^{3})
would still remain unclear.
Dealing with
k
around
\sqrt{n}
has been always challenging in the literature on USD (e.g.,
[
4
, Section 3.3]
).

(2) Limitations of the
k>\sqrt{n}
techniques for 3-Majority.

USD behaves similarly to the well-known consensus dynamics 3-Majority, which has been analyzed extensively for the
entire
range of
2\leq k\leq n

[
6
,
7
,
25
,
10
,
14
,
31
]
.
In 3-Majority, a selected vertex chooses three neighbors uniformly at random and takes the majority opinion held by the three, breaking the tie in favor of the third selected neighbor.
However, several structural and probabilistic features of USD make its analysis, especially in the regime
k\gg\sqrt{n}
, substantially more difficult.

We next explain why all known techniques that successfully handle the
large-
k
regime in 3-Majority fail for USD.
These approaches fall into two broad categories:

•

Coupling + majorization (
[
10
,
14
]
).

The successful analyses for 3-Majority by
[
10
,
14
]
rely on a coupling with Pull Voting
together with a majorization argument to show that the number of surviving opinions rapidly drops to
O(\sqrt{n})
,
after which the concentration-based analysis becomes effective.

•

Potential-based analysis (
[
31
]
).
Another recent approach by
[
31
]
is to design a potential function that exhibits an additive drift and keeps growing during the process.
When the potential function reaches a certain level, the concentration-based analysis becomes effective.

Neither of the above two approaches transfers to USD.
Unlike 3-Majority, USD cannot be related to Pull Voting through coupling
due to the existence of undecided vertices.
At the same time, the potential function used in
[
31
]
itself does not exhibit the additive drift property in USD, again due to undecided vertices.
Specifically, the potential function used in
[
31
]
is defined by

\displaystyle\gamma_{t}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)^{2}.

(2)

Since
\alpha_{t}(i)
given the configuration at round
t-1
can be written as the sum of
n
independent random variables (in the gossip model), a standard moment calculation yields that, for 3-Majority,

\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]=\gamma_{t-1}+\Omega(1/n),

where
\E_{t-1}[\cdot]
is the expectation conditioned on the configuration at round
t-1
.
However, in USD, again due to the emergence of undecided vertices, this inequality does not hold in general.

In summary, although several structural insights from previous work remain conceptually
useful
(e.g., the general ideas of potential-based arguments and concentration-based analysis), none of the existing analytical frameworks transfers to USD in a black-box way.
However, as shown in
Section

2.4
, we can design another potential function
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
that does exhibit an additive drift and keeps growing during the process.

2.3
Proof of
Lemma

2.1
(I): Behavior of
\beta_{t}
and
\psi_{t}
(
Section

5.2
)

Now we outline the proof of the first item of
Lemma

2.1
, which states that (i)
\beta_{t}
becomes
1/2-o(1)
with high probability within
O(\log n)
rounds and remains at least
1/2-o(1)
thereafter, and (ii)
\psi_{t}
is small with high probability for all
t\geq 1
, where
\psi_{t}
is defined in
Eq.

1

and
measures the
change of
\beta_{t}
from round
t-1
to
t
since

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]

\displaystyle=2\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1})+\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t-1}(i)^{2}

(3)

\displaystyle=\beta_{t-1}-\psi_{t-1}.

(4)

For example, if
\alpha_{0}(i)=1/k
for all
i\in[k]
(i.e., the initial configuration is balanced), then
\psi_{0}=1-1/k
, which means that
\beta_{1}
becomes
\beta_{0}-\psi_{0}=1/k
in expectation, i.e.,
\beta_{1}
drops significantly.

Interestingly, as shown in
Lemma

4.6
, after the first round
the expectation of
\psi_{t}
is always small:

\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]\leq\frac{1}{n}.

Combined this with a concentration inequality, we obtain the “Moreover” part of
Lemma

2.1
(I).
Thus, once
\beta_{t}
becomes
1/2-o(1)
, it remains at least
1/2-o(1)
thereafter with high probability.

It remains to show that
\beta_{t}
becomes
1/2-o(1)
within
O(\log n)
rounds with high probability.
Suppose that
\beta_{t-1}=o(1)
. Then, by
Eq.

3
, we have
\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]\geq(2-o(1))\beta_{t-1}
.
Therefore,
\beta_{t}
grows by a factor of
1+\Omega(1)
in each round, which ensures that
\beta_{t}
becomes
\Omega(1)
within
O(\log n)
rounds with high probability.
A similar argument yields that we can show that
\beta_{t}=1/2-o(1)
within
O(\log n)
rounds with high probability.

2.4
Proof of
Lemma

2.1
(II): Growth of
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
(
Sections

5.4
and
5.3
)

We now outline the proof of the second item of
Lemma

2.1
.
The key idea is to normalize the potential
\gamma_{t}
of
Eq.

2
used for 3-Majority by
\beta_{t}^{2}
to obtain the potential
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
defined by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[k]}\left(\frac{\alpha_{t}(i)}{\beta_{t}}\right)^{\!2},&\beta_{t}>0,\\[4.30554pt]
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

A reader familiar with the literature might notice that this quantity is somewhat similar to the
monochromatic distance
introduced in
[
5
]
.
In our notation, the monochromatic distance is defined in
[
5
]
by

\mathrm{md}_{t}:=\sum_{i\in[k]}\quantity( \frac{\alpha_t(i)}{\npm_t} )^{2}.

The difference between
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
and
\mathrm{md}_{t}
is that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
is normalized by
\beta_{t}^{2}
while
\mathrm{md}_{t}
is normalized by
(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t})^{2}
.
This slight modification allows us to evaluate the expected growth of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
, although
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
is in a more complex form compared to
\gamma_{t}
.
Using
Lemma

2.1
(I) that ensures
\beta_{t-1}=\Omega(1)
and a Taylor approximation, we can show that the potential
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
has an additive drift
(
Lemma

4.8
):

\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]\approx\frac{\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]}{\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]^{2}}\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\Omega(1/n).

Using a variant of the optional stopping theorem, we can show that the potential
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
reaches
\Omega((\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
within
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
rounds with high probability.

Once
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
reaches
\Omega((\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
, we have

\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)^{2}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cdot\underbrace{\beta_{t}^{2}}_{\Omega(1)\text{ from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:main goal}(I)}}\geq\Omega\quantity((\log n)^2/\sqrt{n}).

We then show that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
does not decrease too much thereafter.
To this end, we introduce the normalized quantity

\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}/\beta_{t},&\beta_{t}>0,\\
0,&\beta_{t}=0,\end{cases}

which is well-behaved because
\beta_{t}=\Omega(1)
throughout this part of the process.
In
Lemma

4.7
, we will prove

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}]\geq\left(1-O\quantity(\frac 1 n)\right)\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1},

(5)

which ensures that once
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
reaches
\Omega((\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
, it remains above this threshold (up to a constant factor) for
\Theta(n)
rounds with high probability.

2.5
Proof of
Lemma

2.1
(III): Gap between Two Opinions (
Sections

5.5
and
5.6
)

Recall that an opinion
i
is weak at round
t
if
\alpha_{t}(i)\leq 0.9\cdot\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and strong at round
t
if
\alpha_{t}(i)\geq 0.95\cdot\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
.
Fix two distinct non-weak opinions
i,j\in[k]
.
The goal is to show that (i) either
i
or
j
becomes weak within
O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds with high probability, and (ii) once an opinion becomes weak, it cannot become strong during the rest of the process.

To this end, we are interested in the gap between
i
and
j
, which is defined as

\delta_{t}:=\alpha_{t}(i)-\alpha_{t}(j).

Fix a round
t-1\geq 1
.
In
Lemma

4.4
, we will show that the expectation of
\delta_{t}
given
\alpha_{t-1}
is

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]=\delta_{t-1}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)+2(1-\beta_{t-1})).

(6)

Our aim is to show that
\delta_{t}
grows by a factor of
1+\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1})
in each round.
However, this does not hold in general.
For example, if
\beta_{t-1}=1
and
\alpha_{t-1}(i)=\Theta(1/k)
for all
i\in[k]
, then a standard Chernoff bound argument yields that
\alpha_{t}(i)\approx\alpha_{t-1}(i)^{2}=\Theta(1/k^{2})
, yielding
\delta_{t}\ll\delta_{t-1}
.
Thus we need to put some assumptions on the configuration at round
t-1
, which
are listed below:

•

Suppose that both
i
and
j
are non-weak at round
t-1
. Then, we have
\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)\geq 1.8\cdot\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}
.

•

Suppose that
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-o(1)
and
\psi_{t-1}\leq o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, which holds from
Lemma

2.1
(I).
Then, since
\psi_{t-1}=\beta_{t-1}(2\beta_{t-1}-1)-\gamma_{t-1}
and

\gamma_{t-1}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{t-1}(i)^{2}\leq\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\cdot\sum_{i}\alpha_{t-1}(i)=\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\cdot\beta_{t-1}
, we have

\displaystyle 2\beta_{t-1}-1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{t-1}+o(\log n/\sqrt{n})}{\beta_{t-1}}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}+o(\log n/\sqrt{n}).

Applying these two items to
Eq.

6
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{t-1}\quantity(1 + \underbrace{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)}_{\ge 1.8\cdot\npm_{t-1}}-\underbrace{(2\beta_{t-1}-1)}_{\le\npm_{t-1} + o(\log n/\sqrt{n})})

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{t-1}\quantity(1 + 0.8\cdot\npm_{t-1} - o(\log n/\sqrt{n}))

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{t-1}\quantity(1+\Omega(\npm_{t-1})).

(7)

In the last inequality, note that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\geq\Omega((\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
by the assumption.

Finally, from
Eq.

5
and
\beta_{t}=\Omega(1)
, we have that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
for all
t=O(n)
(in expectation).
Thus, we obtain

\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]\geq\delta_{t-1}\quantity(1+\Omega(\npm_{0})).

Even if we start with
\delta_{0}=0
(i.e.,
\alpha_{0}(i)=\alpha_{0}(j)
), by calculation (see
Lemma

4.4
), we have that its second moment satisfies

\Var_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]=\Omega\quantity(\frac{(1-\beta_{t-1})^2(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j))}{n})=\Omega\quantity(\frac{\npm_0}{n}),

and in particular

\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{2}]\geq\delta_{t-1}^{2}+\Omega\quantity(\frac{\npm_0}{n}).

Thus, by the optional stopping theorem combined with a widely-known argument from
[
17
]
, we can show that
\absolutevalue{\delta_t}
becomes at least
\Omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
within
O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds with high probability.
Therefore, within
O(\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds, either
i
or
j
becomes weak with high probability.

2.6
Concentration Bounds via Freedman’s Inequality

To make the analysis of
Sections

2.5
and
2.4
rigorous,
we require concentration bounds for several stochastic quantities (e.g.,
\delta_{t}
).
Our approach follows the Freedman-based framework developed in

[
14
,
31
]
, which applies Freedman’s inequality (a Bernstein-type concentration inequality for martingales) to obtain tight concentration bounds for various quantities.

In the population protocol model, these inequalities apply directly because
each update affects only one vertex.
In the gossip model, however, a synchronous round aggregates
n
independent
local changes.
Based on the observation that the one-step difference (e.g.,
\alpha_{t}(i)-\alpha_{t-1}(i)
) can be written as the sum of
n
independent random variables,
[
31
]
introduced the concept of
Bernstein condition
, which is a sufficient condition for the application of Freedman’s inequality even
without bounded single-step jumps; see
Section

3.2
.

However, the framework of
[
31
]
used for 3-Majority is insufficient for USD.
For 3-Majority, the quantities of interest
(e.g.,
\alpha_{t}(i)
or
\delta_{t}=\alpha_{t}(i)-\alpha_{t}(j)
) are linear in
independent random variables, which allows us to write the one-step difference as the sum of independent random variables.
In USD, however, the presence of undecided vertices destroys the clean drift
structure of
\alpha_{t}(i)
, forcing us to analyze normalized
quantities such as

\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=\frac{\|\alpha_{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{\beta_{t}^{2}},\qquad\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}=\frac{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}}{\beta_{t}}.

While
\beta_{t}
concentrates reasonably well, its variance typically
dominates that of
\alpha_{t}(i)
, so concentration of the numerator and the denominator

separately
does not yield tight bounds for their ratio.
Thus, the straightforward application of the Freedman-based framework is insufficient in its current form.

To overcome this difficulty, we apply a first-order Taylor expansion of
1/\beta_{t}
around
\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]
:

\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\geq\frac{1}{\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]}-\frac{\beta_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]}{\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]^{2}}.

Multiplying by
\alpha_{t}(i)
gives

\frac{\alpha_{t}(i)}{\beta_{t}}\geq\frac{\alpha_{t}(i)}{\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]}-\frac{\alpha_{t}(i)}{\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]^{2}}\,(\beta_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]).

Both
\alpha_{t}(i)
and
\beta_{t}
are sums of
n
independent random variables,
so the right-hand side becomes a quadratic form in such sums.
Using read-
k
concentration bounds
[
24
,
18
]
, we obtain sharp
concentration for these quadratic forms, and hence for the normalized
quantities themselves.

This resolves the main obstacle preventing the direct use of
Freedman-style arguments for USD and provides the concentration guarantees
needed throughout the analysis.

3
Definitions and Main Analysis Tools

3.1
Model and Basic Notation

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.
For
p>0
and a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\norm{x}_{p}=\quantity(\sum_{i=1}^n |x_i|^p)^{1/p}
denote the
\ell^{p}
-norm of
x
.
For a finite set
S
, by
x\sim S
we mean that
x
is chosen uniformly at random from
S
.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we use the shorthand
a\land b=\min\{a,b\}
.
We denote by
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}
the indicator random variable of an event
\mathcal{E}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}=1
if
\mathcal{E}
occurs and
0
otherwise.

Throughout the paper, we fix a set of
n
vertices, denoted by
V
, with
|V|=n
.
Each vertex holds an opinion from a finite alphabet
\Sigma=[k]\cup\{\bot\}
,
where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a parameter and
\bot
represents the undecided state.
All configurations of the system are elements of
\Sigma^{V}
.
The interaction rule (gossip or population protocol) will be specified later,
but in all cases the opinion update is governed by the same USD update rule
introduced below.

Definition 3.1

(USD Update Rule)
.

The
USD update rule
is a function
\mathsf{update}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\Sigma
defined by

\mathsf{update}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\begin{cases}\bot&\text{if $|\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\bot\}|=3$},\\
\sigma_{2}&\text{if $\sigma_{1}=\bot$},\\
\sigma_{1}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that, the condition
|\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\bot\}|=3
means that both
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are decided and they are distinct.

Using the update rule above,
we define USD in the gossip model.
In this model, every vertex updates simultaneously in each round by interacting with an independently chosen random neighbor.

Definition 3.2

(Gossip USD)
.

The
gossip USD
is the discrete-time Markov chain
(\mathrm{opn}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on the state space
\Sigma^{V}
.
Given
\mathrm{opn}_{t}\in\Sigma^{V}
, the next configuration
\mathrm{opn}_{t+1}\in\Sigma^{V}
is obtained by the following procedure:
For each vertex
u\in V
, independently select a random vertex
v\sim V
and set

\mathrm{opn}_{t+1}(u)=\mathsf{update}(\mathrm{opn}_{t}(u),\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v))
.

We next define the Undecided-State Dynamics (USD) under the population protocol model.
Here, time evolves through a sequence of pairwise interactions:
in each step, a single ordered pair of vertices is sampled uniformly at random, and the initiator
(the first vertex in the pair) updates its state using the USD update rule.

Definition 3.3

(Population Protocol USD)
.

The
population protocol USD
is the discrete-time Markov chain
(\mathrm{opn}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on the state space
\Sigma^{V}
.
Given
\mathrm{opn}_{t}\in\Sigma^{V}
, the next configuration
\mathrm{opn}_{t+1}\in\Sigma^{V}
is obtained by the following procedure:
Select two uniformly random vertices
u,v\sim V
with replacement and set

\mathrm{opn}_{t+1}(u)=\mathsf{update}(\mathrm{opn}_{t}(u),\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v))
.
All vertices other than
u
keep their opinions unchanged,
i.e.,
\mathrm{opn}_{t+1}(w)=\mathrm{opn}_{t}(w)
for all
w\neq u
.

Remark 3.4
.

We use the same notation
\mathrm{opn}_{t}
for configurations in both models.
The ambient model is always clear from context, whereas introducing
model-specific symbols such as
\mathrm{opn}_{t}^{\mathrm{gossip}}
or

\mathrm{opn}_{t}^{\mathrm{pp}}
would only create unnecessary notational branching
without improving clarity.

The main performance measure of USD is the
consensus time
, defined by

\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}=\inf\quantity{t\ge 0 \colon\exists a\in[k],\,\forall u\in V, \opn_t(u)=a}.

Note that
\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}=\infty
if for some
t\geq 0
,
\mathrm{opn}_{t}(u)=\bot
for all
u\in V
.

We sometimes use
\mathcal{F}_{t}
to denote the natural filtration generated by the process
(\mathrm{opn}_{s})_{s\leq t}
.
Using this notation, we often use abbreviations
\Pr_{t-1}[\cdot]
,
\E_{t-1}[\cdot]
, and
\Var_{t-1}[\cdot]
for
\Pr[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
,
\E[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
, and
\Var[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
, respectively.
For a random variable
X_{t}
defined for step
t
in USD, we often use the term “
X_{t}
conditioned on round
t-1
”
to denote this random variable conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.

In
Section

4
, we will define the quantities of interest mentioned in
Section

2
and review their basic properties.

3.2
Bernstein Condition

The Bernstein condition
[
31
]
provides uniform control on the moment generating
function of a random variable and will be used throughout to obtain
concentration bounds for the quantities appearing in USD, such as

\alpha_{t}(i)
and their normalized forms.

Definition 3.5

(Bernstein condition;
[
31
]
)
.

Let
D,s\geq 0
.
A random variable
X
satisfies the
(D,s)
-Bernstein condition if
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
with
|\lambda|D<3
,

\E[e^{\lambda X}]\leq\exp\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}s/2}{1-|\lambda|D/3}\right).

It satisfies the
one-sided

(D,s)
-Bernstein condition if the
same bound holds for all
\lambda\geq 0
with
\lambda D<3
.

The one-sided condition yields the following standard tail bound
(see, e.g.,
[
32
, Proposition 2.14]
):

Remark 3.6
.

If
X
satisfies the one-sided
(D,s)
-Bernstein condition, then
for all
h\geq 0
,
using
\Pr\left[e^{\lambda X}\geq e^{\lambda h}\right]\leq e^{-\lambda h}\E[e^{\lambda X}]
and
the bound on
\E[e^{\lambda X}]
, and taking
\lambda=h/(s+hD/3)
, we get

\Pr[X\geq h]\leq\exp\quantity(
-\frac{h^2/2}{s + (hD)/3}
).

We record the basic closure properties used repeatedly later
(
[
31
, Lemma 3.4]
).

Lemma 3.7

(Basic properties of Bernstein condition;
[
31
]
)
.

Let
X,Y
be random variables. We have the following:

(i)

If
\E[X]=0
and
\absolutevalue{X}\leq D
, then
X
satisfies
D,\quantity(\bounded,\Var\qty[X])\quantity[X]
-Bernstein condition.

(ii)

If
X
satisfies
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition, then

X
satisfies
\quantity(\bounded',\variance')
-Bernstein condition for any

D^{\prime}\geq D
and
s^{\prime}\geq s
(same for the one-sided version).

(iii)

If
X
satisfies
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition, then
aX
satisfies
(\absolutevalue{a}D,a^{2}s)
-Bernstein condition for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
(same for the one-sided version if
a\geq 0
).

(iv)

If
X
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition and
Y\preceq X
,
then
Y
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded, \variance)
-Bernstein condition, where
\preceq
denotes the stochastic domination (see
Definition

A.5
).

(v)

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are independent and each
X_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition, then
\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i)
-Bernstein condition.

(vi)

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are negatively associated and each
X_{i}
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition, then
\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i}
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(D,\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i)
-Bernstein condition.

We will need Bernstein bounds for sums of
random variables that may not be independent, namely, the sum of a read-
\ell
family (
Definition

A.2
).

Lemma 3.8
.

We have the following:

(i)

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are random variables such that each
X_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition, then
\sum_{i\in[n]}X_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(nD, n\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i)
-Bernstein condition.

(ii)

If
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
is a read-
\ell
family and each
Y_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition, then the sum
\sum_{i\in[n]}Y_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(\ell D, \ell\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i)
-Bernstein condition.

Proof of
Item

1
.

Since
X_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition,
nX_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(nD, n^2s_i)
-Bernstein condition from
Item

3
of
Lemma

3.7
,
i.e., for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\absolutevalue{\lambda}nD<3
, we have
\E\quantity[\e^{\lambda nX_i}]\leq\exp\quantity(\frac{\lambda^2 n^2s_i/2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} n\bounded)/3})
.
Consider
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\absolutevalue{\lambda}nD<3
.
For such
\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\E\quantity[\prod_{i\in[n]}\e^{\lambda X_i}]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[n]}\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]}\E\qty[\e^{\lambda X_i n}])\quantity[\e^{\lambda X_i n}]^{1/n}

\displaystyle(\text{From H\"{o}lder's inequality})

\displaystyle\leq\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]}\exp\qty(\frac{\lambda^2 n^2s_i / 2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} n\bounded)/3}))^{1/n}

\displaystyle=\exp\quantity(\frac{\lambda^2 n\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i / 2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} n\bounded)/3}).

∎

Proof of
Item

2
.

Since
Y_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(D,s_i)
-Bernstein condition,
\ell Y_{i}
satisfies
\quantity(\ell D, \ell^2s_i)
-Bernstein condition from
Item

3
of
Lemma

3.7
,
i.e., for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\absolutevalue{\lambda}\ell D<3
, we have
\E\quantity[\e^{\lambda\ell Y_i}]\leq\exp\quantity(\frac{\lambda^2 \ell^2s_i/2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} \ell\bounded)/3})
.
Consider
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\absolutevalue{\lambda}\ell D<3
.
For such
\lambda
, we have

\displaystyle\E\quantity[\prod_{i\in[n]}\e^{\lambda Y_i}]

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i\in[n]}\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]}\E\qty[\e^{\lambda Y_i \ell}])\quantity[\e^{\lambda Y_i \ell}]^{1/\ell}

\displaystyle(\text{From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:moment of read-k family}})

\displaystyle\leq\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]}\exp\qty(\frac{\lambda^2 \ell^2s_i / 2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} \ell\bounded)/3}))^{1/\ell}

\displaystyle=\exp\quantity(\frac{\lambda^2 \ell\sum_{i\in[n]}s_i / 2}{1-(\abs{\lambda} \ell\bounded)/3}).

∎

3.3
Freedman’s Inequality and Drift Analysis

Drift analysis allows us to bound the hitting time of a stochastic process
once we control both its expected evolution and the concentration of its
increments. We briefly recall the basic mechanism.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a process with

\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\geq X_{t-1}+R\qquad\text{whenever }X_{t-1}<a,

for some
R>0
and threshold
a>X_{0}
,
and define the stopping time

\tau:=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq a\}.

Consider the stopped process

Y_{t}:=X_{t\wedge\tau}-R\,(t\wedge\tau),

which is a submartingale.
If the increments
Y_{t}-Y_{t-1}
satisfy a Bernstein condition,
Freedman’s inequality yields, with high probability,

Y_{T}\geq Y_{0}-\varepsilon=X_{0}-\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all sufficiently large }T.

On the event
\{T<\tau\}
, we have
X_{T}<a
and hence

X_{T}-RT=Y_{T}\geq X_{0}-\varepsilon,

which implies

\displaystyle T\leq\frac{a-X_{0}+\varepsilon}{R}.

(8)

Thus
\tau
is at most this value with high probability.
This is the standard way in which positive drift and concentration combine
to yield upper bounds on hitting times; see, e.g.,
[
30
]
.
The following lemma, introduced in
[
31
]
, summarizes the above discussion, which is based on the Freedman’s inequality.

Lemma 3.9

(Lemma 3.5 of
[
31
]
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of random variables and
let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a filtration such that
X_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
t\geq 0
.
Let
\tau
be a stopping time with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
Let
D,s\geq 0
and
R\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters.
Suppose the following condition holds for any
t\geq 1
: conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
,

(C1)

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\left\lparen\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t-1}-R\right\rparen\leq 0
,

(C2)

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\left\lparen X_{t}-X_{t-1}-R\right\rparen
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition.

For a parameter
h>0
, define stopping times

\displaystyle\tau_{X}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0\colon X_t\geq X_0+h}\text{ and }\tau_{X}^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0\colon X_t\leq X_0-h}.

Then, we have the following:

(i)

Suppose
R\geq 0
. Then, for any
h,T>0
such that
z\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=h-R\cdot T>0
, we have

\Pr\quantity[\tauxplus\le\min\{T,\tau\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{z^2/2}{\variance T + (z \bounded)/3}).

(ii)

Suppose
R<0
. Then, for any
h,T>0
such that
z\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(-R)\cdot T-h>0
, we have

\Pr\quantity[\min\cbra{\tauxminus,\tau} >T]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{z^2/2}{\variance T + (z \bounded)/3}).

Remark 3.10
.

Since
Item

(

⁢
C
1

)

implies
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\left\lparen X_{t}-X_{t-1}-R\right\rparen\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\left\lparen X_{t}-\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\right\rparen
, we can use the following
Item

(

⁢
C

2
′

)

instead of
Item

(

⁢
C
2

)

:

(C2^{\prime})

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\left\lparen X_{t}-\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\right\rparen
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition.

In this paper, we frequently use the following lemma, which is directly derived from
Lemma

3.9
and is formulated for convenience in our applications.
The proof is deferred to
Section

A.1
.
Roughly speaking, the result concerns a quantity
X_{t}
of interest that satisfies the Bernstein condition and ensures that the following hold (with a sufficiently large probability):

•

If for all
t\geq 1
,
\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\geq X_{t-1}+R
for some
R<0
(i.e.,
\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\geq X_{t-1}-r
for some
r>0
), then
X_{t}>I^{-}
holds for all
t\leq(1-\varepsilon)\frac{X_{0}-I^{-}}{-R}
(
=(1-\varepsilon)\frac{X_{0}-I^{-}}{r}
).
In other words,
X_{t}
does not decrease too much if
\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]
is not too small compared to
X_{t-1}
. For example, we apply this claim for
X_{t}=\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
, which may have a tiny negative drift
Eq.

5
.

•

If for all
t\geq 1
,
\E_{t-1}[X_{t}]\geq X_{t-1}+R
for some
R>0
, then
X_{T}\geq I^{+}
holds for some
T\geq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{I^{+}-X_{0}}{R}
. In other words,
X_{t}
increases rapidly if it exhibits a positive drift. For example, we apply this claim for
X_{t}=\delta_{t}
, turning a positive multiplicative drift given in
Eq.

7
into an additive drift

\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]\geq\delta_{t-1}+\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\delta_{0})
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of random variables and
let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a filtration such that
X_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
t\geq 0
.
Let
\tau^{*}
be a stopping time with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
For parameters
I^{-}
and
I^{+}
, define stopping times

\displaystyle\tau^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0\colon X_t\geq I^+}\text{ and }\tau^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0\colon X_t\leq I^-}.

Then, we have the following:

1.

Suppose the conditions
Items

(

⁢
C
1

)

and

(

⁢
C
2

)

are valid for parameters
D,s\geq 0
,
R<0
and
\tau=\tau^{*}
.
Then, for any positive constant
\varepsilon>0
, and any
T\leq\frac{(1-\varepsilon)(X_{0}-I^{-})}{-R}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^- \le\min\{T,\tau^*\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{\epsilon^2(X_0-I^-)^2/2}{\variance T + (\epsilon(X_0-I^-)\bounded)/3}).

2.

Suppose the conditions
Items

(

⁢
C
1

)

and

(

⁢
C
2

)

are valid for parameters
D,s\geq 0
,
R>0
and
\tau=\min\{\tau^{+},\tau^{-},\tau^{*}\}
.
Then, for any positive constant
\varepsilon>0
and any
T\geq\frac{(1+\varepsilon)(I^{+}-X_{0})}{R}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>T \text{ and } \tau^*>T]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{(X_0-I^-)^2/2}{\variance T + ((X_0-I^-) \bounded)/3})+\exp\quantity(- \frac{\epsilon^2(I^+-X_0)^2/2}{\variance T + (\epsilon(I^+-X_0)\bounded)/3}),

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>\tau^- \text{ and } \tau^*>T]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{(X_0-I^-)^2/2}{\variance T + ((X_0-I^-) \bounded)/3})+\exp\quantity(- \frac{\epsilon^2(I^+-X_0)^2/2}{\variance T + (\epsilon(I^+-X_0)\bounded)/3}).

4
Key Quantities

In this section, we introduce the quantities that will be used throughout our
analysis of USD. For each quantity, we establish its conditional expectation,
conditional variance, and a one-sided Bernstein condition. These properties
form the backbone of the drift arguments used later.

Since the definitions are intentionally consolidated here, the presentation is
dense; readers are encouraged to refer back to this section as needed when
following the proofs in
Sections

5
and
6
.

Definition 4.1

(Key Quantities)
.

Let
(\mathrm{opn}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be either the gossip USD or the population protocol USD.
For each round
t\geq 0
, define:

•

For each
i\in[k]
, let
\alpha_{t}(i):=\frac{1}{n}\absolutevalue{\{u\in V : \opn_t(u)=i\}}
, and let
\beta_{t}:=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)
denote the total fraction of vertices holding a decided opinion.

•

\gamma_{t}:=\norm{\alpha_t}_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)^{2}
.

•

\psi_{t}:=\beta_{t}(2\beta_{t}-1)-\gamma_{t}
.

•

\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}:=\gamma_{t}/\beta_{t}^{2}
. If
\beta_{t}=0
, we define
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=0
.

•

\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}:=\norm{\alpha_t}_{\infty}=\max_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t}(i)
.

•

\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}:=\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}/\beta_{t}
. If
\beta_{t}=0
, we define
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}=0
.

•

For
i,j\in[k]
and
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
, define
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}(i,j)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\alpha_{t}(i)-(1+\varepsilon)\alpha_{t}(j)
and
\delta_{t}(i,j)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{t}^{(0)}(i,j)
. If
i
and
j
are clear from the context, we write
\delta_{t}
or
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}
instead of
\delta_{t}(i,j)
or
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}(i,j)
, respectively.

•

Let
I_{t}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\min\{i\in[k]:\alpha_{t}(i)=\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\}
be the most popular opinion at round
t
.
For
i\in[k]
, let
\eta_{t}(i):=\delta_{t}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t},i)=\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}-(1+c_{\eta})\alpha_{t}(i)
, where the constant
c_{\eta}>0
is fixed in
Definition

5.15
.

Throughout this paper, we shall use
\E_{t-1}[\cdot]
to denote the expectation conditioned on
round
t-1
.
For example,
\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}]
is the expectation of
\alpha_{t}
conditioned on
round
t-1
.

4.1
Basic Properties in the Gossip Model

We now present the basic properties (expectations, variances, and Bernstein conditions) of the key quantities introduced in
Definition

4.1
in the gossip model.
These results are applied repeatedly in the phase analysis.
All proofs of the lemmas in this section are deferred to
Appendix

B
,
as they are routine calculations or applications of lemmas in
Section

3.2
.

Lemma 4.2

(Basic properties for
\alpha_{t}(i)
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\alpha_{t}(i)
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)

\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]=\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+2\qty(1-\beta_{t-1}))
.

2.

(Variance)

\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}\quantity[ (1-\beta_{t-1})(1+\beta_{t-1}-2\alpha_{t-1}(i))+\alpha_{t-1}(i)(\beta_{t-1}-\alpha_{t-1}(i))]
.
Specifically,
\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\leq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}
and
\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\geq\frac{\quantity(1-\beta_{t-1})^{2}\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}
hold.

3.

(Bernstein condition)

\alpha_{t}(i)-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{1}{n},\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.3

(Basic properties for
\beta_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\beta_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]=2\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}=\beta_{t-1}-\psi_{t-1}
.

2.

(Variance)
\Var_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]=\frac{(\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1})(1-\beta_{t-1}+\gamma_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}^{2}-\norm{\alpha_{t-1}}_{3}^{3}}{n}
.
In particular,
\Var_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]\leq\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{n}
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)
\beta_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity( \frac{1}{n}, \frac{\beta_{t-1}}{n} )
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.4

(Basic properties for
\delta^{(\varepsilon)}_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}(i,j)
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)

\E_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t^{(\epsilon)}]=\delta_{t-1}^{(\varepsilon)}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)+2(1-\beta_{t-1}))+\varepsilon\alpha_{t-1}(i)\alpha_{t-1}(j)
. In particular,
\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]=\delta_{t-1}+\delta_{t-1}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)}{\npm_{t-1}}-\frac{\gamma_{t-1}+\psi_{t-1}}{\beta_{t-1}\npm_{t-1}})
.

2.

(Variance)

\Var_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t]\geq\frac{(1-\beta_{t-1})^{2}}{n}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j))
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)
The difference
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}-\E_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t^{(\epsilon)}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies

\quantity(\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{n},s)
-Bernstein condition for
s=\frac{2}{n}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+(1+\epsilon)^2\alpha_{t-1}(j))

Lemma 4.5

(Basic properties for
\gamma_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\gamma_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation Upper Bound)
\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]\leq 10\gamma_{t-1}
.

2.

(Expectation Lower Bound)
\beta_{t-1}^{2}\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]\geq\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]^{2}\gamma_{t-1}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}^{3}(1-\beta_{t-1})^{2}}{n}
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)
\gamma_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{2}{n}, \frac{20\gamma_{t-1}}{n})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.6

(Basic properties for
\psi_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\psi_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]\leq\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{n}
.

2.

(Bernstein condition)
\psi_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity( \frac{24}{n}, \frac{400\beta_{t-1}}{n} )
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.7

(Basic properties for
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
satisfies the following:
Let
C>0
be a sufficiently large constant.
Suppose that
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-o(1)
and
\psi_{t-1}\leq o(1)
. Then,

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}]\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\npt_{t-1}/\npo_{t-1}}{2(1-\npo_{t-1})+\npt_{t-1}/\npo_{t-1}})-\frac{9\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n}
.

2.

(Bernstein condition)
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\npt_{t-1}/\npo_{t-1}}{2(1-\npo_{t-1})+\npt_{t-1}/\npo_{t-1}})-\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}-\frac{9\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n}
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\frac{C}{n}, \frac{C\npm_{t-1}}{n})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.8

(Basic properties for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
in the gossip model)
.

For the gossip USD, the quantity
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
satisfies the following:
Suppose
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-o(1)
,
\psi_{t-1}\leq o(1)
, and
\gamma_{t-1}\leq o(1)
. Then,
\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{12n}
.

4.2
Basic Properties in the Population Protocol Model

We now present the basic properties (expectations, variances, and Bernstein conditions) of the key quantities introduced in
Definition

4.1
in the population protocol model, which are analogous of
Section

4.1
.
The proofs can be found in
Section

B.2
.

Lemma 4.9

(Basic properties for
\alpha_{t}(i)
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\alpha_{t}(i)
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)

\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]=\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(1+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+1-2\beta_{t-1}}{n})
.

2.

(Variance)

\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n^{2}}\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(i)-\alpha_{t-1}(i)\qty(1-2\beta_{t-1}+\alpha_{t-1}(i))^2)
.
In particular,
\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\leq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)

\alpha_{t}(i)-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{1}{n},\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n^2})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.10

(Basic properties for
\beta_{t}
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\beta_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}\quantity[\beta_t]=\beta_{t-1}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}\quantity(1-2\beta_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}}{n}
.

2.

(Variance)
\Var_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]\leq\frac{\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}}{n^{2}}
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)
\beta_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity( \frac{1}{n}, \frac{\beta_{t-1}}{n^2} )
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.11

(Basic properties for
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}(i,j)

satisfy the following:

1.

(Expectation)

\E_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t^{(\epsilon)}]=\delta_{t-1}^{(\varepsilon)}\quantity(1+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)+1-2\beta_{t-1}}{n})+\frac{\varepsilon\alpha_{t-1}(i)\alpha_{t-1}(j)}{n}
.

2.

(Variance)

\Var_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t]\geq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(i))}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(j)\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(j))}{n^{2}}-\frac{4\delta_{t-1}^{2}}{n^{2}}.

3.

(Bernstein condition)

\delta_{t}^{(\varepsilon)}-\E_{t-1}\quantity[\delta_t^{(\epsilon)}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies

\quantity(\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{n},s)
-Bernstein condition for
s=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+(1+\epsilon)^2\alpha_{t-1}(j))
.

Lemma 4.12

(Basic properties for
\gamma_{t}
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\gamma_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]=\gamma_{t-1}+\frac{2}{n}\quantity((1-2\beta_{t-1})\gamma_{t-1}+\norm{\alpha_{t-1}}_3^3)+\frac{\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}}{n^{2}}
.

2.

(Variance)
\Var_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]\leq\frac{9\norm{\alpha_{t-1}}_{3}^{3}}{n^{2}}
, where
\norm{\alpha_{t-1}}_{3}^{3}=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t-1}(i)^{3}
.

3.

(Bernstein condition)
\gamma_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{8\npm_{t-1}}{n},\frac{9\norm{\alpha_{t-1}}_3^3
}{n^2})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.13

(Basic properties for
\psi_{t}
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\psi_{t}
satisfies the following:

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]\leq\psi_{t-1}\quantity(1-\frac{1}{n})+\frac{\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}}{n^{2}}
.

2.

(Bernstein condition)
\psi_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies
\quantity( \frac{150}{n}, \frac{150\beta_{t-1}}{n^2} )
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.14

(Basic properties for
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
in the population protocol model)
.

For the population protocol USD, the quantity
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
satisfies the following:
Let
C>0
be a sufficiently large constant. Suppose
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-o(1)
. Then,

1.

(Expectation)
\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpm_t]\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\frac{18\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n^{2}}
.

2.

(Bernstein condition)

\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}-\frac{18\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n^{2}}
conditioned on round
t-1
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\frac{C}{n}, \frac{C\npm_{t-1}}{n^2})
-Bernstein condition.

Lemma 4.15

(Basic properties for
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
in the population protocol model)
.

Suppose that
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-o(1)
and
\gamma_{t-1}\leq o(1)
.
Then for the population protocol USD,
\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_t]\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{12n^{2}}
.

4.3
Stopping Times

In the remainder of the paper we frequently refer to the first time at which
one of the key quantities introduced above crosses a given threshold.
Since these stopping times are used uniformly in both the gossip model
and the population–protocol model, we introduce them here in a unified way.

For a stochastic process
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and a threshold
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
we consider the upward and downward hitting times of the form

\tau^{+}_{Z}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:Z_{t}\geq\theta\}
and
\tau^{-}_{Z}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:Z_{t}\leq\theta\}
.
We will consider such stopping times for
\beta_{t}
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
,
\delta_{t}
,
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
, etc.
These stopping times serve as canonical milestones in our drift and
concentration arguments later on.

Whenever a stopping time refers to a quantity that depends on a pair of
opinions (e.g.,
\delta_{t}
), the stopping time is understood to be taken
with respect to that fixed pair unless otherwise stated.

Definition 4.16

(Stopping Times)
.

Consider quantities defined in
Definition

4.1
.

•

(Stopping times for
\beta_{t}
) For a parameter
x>0
, define the stopping times

\tau_{\beta}^{+}(x)=\inf\quantity{t\ge 0: \beta_t \ge\frac{1}{2}-x}\text{ and }\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x)=\inf\quantity{t\ge 0: \beta_t < \frac{1}{2}-x}.

By default, we set the parameter
x
to be any positive function
x_{\beta}=x_{\beta}(n)
satisfying
x_{\beta}(n)=\omega\quantity( \sqrt{\log n/n} )
and
x_{\beta}(n)=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
(for example,
x_{\beta}=(\log n)^{3/5}/\sqrt{n}
).
We sometimes abbreviate
\tau_{\beta}^{+}(x_{\beta})
and
\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x_{\beta})
to
\tau_{\beta}^{+}
and
\tau_{\beta}^{-}
, respectively.

•

(Stopping times for
\psi_{t}
) For a parameter
x>0
,
define the stopping times

\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x)=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\psi_t> x}\text{ and }\tau^{-}_{\psi}(x)=\inf\quantity{t\ge 0\colon\psi_t\le x}.

By default, we set the parameter
x
to be a function
x=x_{\psi}(n)
satisfying
x_{\psi}=x_{\beta}/4
.
We sometimes abbreviate
\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x_{\psi})
and
\tau^{-}_{\psi}(x_{\psi})
to
\tau_{\psi}^{+}
and
\tau^{-}_{\psi}
, respectively.

•

(Stopping time for
\gamma_{t}
) For a parameter
x_{\gamma}\in(0,1)
, define the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{\gamma}^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\gamma_t \geq\xgamma}.

Throughout this paper, we fix
x_{\gamma}=(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n}
.

•

(Stopping times for
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
) For constants
c_{\max}^{\uparrow},c_{\max}^{\downarrow}\in(0,1)
, define the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\npm_t\geq(1+\cmaxup)\npm_0},

\displaystyle\tau_{\max}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\npm_t\leq(1-\cmaxdown)\npm_0}.

For positive constants
c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}\in(0,1)
, define the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\tnpm_t\geq(1+\ctildemaxup)\tnpm_0},

\displaystyle\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\tnpm_t\leq(1-\ctildemaxdown)\tnpm_0}.

By default, we set
c_{\max}^{\uparrow}=c_{\max}^{\downarrow}=c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}=c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}=0.1
.

•

(Stopping times for
\delta_{t}
) For constants
c_{\delta}^{\uparrow},c_{\delta}^{\downarrow}\in(0,1)
, define the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\delta_t\geq(1+\cdeltaup)\delta_0}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{\delta}^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0:\delta_t\leq(1-\cdeltadown)\delta_0}.

be the stopping times for
\delta_{t}
growing up and down multiplicatively.
The constants
c_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
and
c_{\delta}^{\downarrow}
are defined as appropriate within the proofs.

For a threshold parameter
x>0
, define the stopping time

\displaystyle\tau_{\delta}^{+}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq 0\colon\abs{\delta_t} \geq x}.

By default, we set the parameter
x
to be
x=c_{\delta}^{+}/\sqrt{n}
, where
c_{\delta}^{+}
is a positive constant defined as appropriate within the proofs.
We sometimes abbreviate
\tau_{\delta}^{+}(x_{\delta})
to
\tau_{\delta}^{+}
, respectively.

•

(Stopping times for
\eta_{t}
)
For a parameter
x>0
and an opinion
j\in[k]
, define the stopping times

\displaystyle\tau^{+}_{\eta}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\eta_{t}(j)\geq x\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{-}_{\eta}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\eta_{t}(j)<x\}.

By default, we set the parameter
x
to be any positive function
x=x_{\eta}(n)
satisfying
x_{\eta}(n)=\omega\quantity( \xbeta(n) )
and
x_{\eta}(n)=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
(for example,
x_{\eta}=(\log n)^{4/5}/\sqrt{n}
).
We sometimes abbreviate
\tau^{+}_{\eta}(x_{\eta})
and
\tau^{-}_{\eta}(x_{\eta})
to
\tau^{+}_{\eta}
and
\tau^{-}_{\eta}
, respectively.

5
Analysis for the Gossip Model

In this section we present the full proof of
Theorem

1.1
for the gossip
model. The structure of the argument follows the outline given in

Section

2
: each subsection implements one of the steps
described there, in the same order and with the corresponding technical tools.
Because every component of the overview requires its own drift and
concentration analysis, the section is necessarily long; however, its
organization mirrors the conceptual roadmap of
Section

2
, and
the reader may consult that discussion to track how each ingredient fits into
the overall proof.

5.1
Probability of Failure at the First Round

In this subsection, we shall consider the configuration after the first synchronous update.
Specifically, we obtain the probability that the dynamics fails at the first round.

Lemma 5.1
.

Consider USD in the gossip model. Let
p_{\bot}=\Pr[\beta_{1}=0]
be the probability that all vertices hold
\bot
after the first round.
Then, we have the following:

1.

If
\beta_{0}=1
, then
p_{\bot}=\prod_{i\in[k]}\quantity(1-\alpha_0(i))^{n\alpha_{0}(i)}\leq\exp(-n\gamma_0)\leq\exp(-n/k)
.

2.

If
0<\beta_{0}<1
, then
\Pr[\beta_{1}=0]\leq\frac{1}{n}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle p_{\bot}

\displaystyle=(1-\beta_{0})^{(1-\beta_{0})n}\cdot\prod_{i\in[k]}(\beta_{0}-\alpha_{0}(i))^{\alpha_{0}(i)n}

(9)

Here, note that
(1-\beta_{0})^{(1-\beta_{0})n}
amounts for the probability that all initially undecided vertices keep
\bot
after the first round,
and
\prod_{i\in[k]}(\beta_{0}-\alpha_{0}(i))^{\alpha_{0}(i)n}
amounts for the probability that all initially decided vertices becomes undecided after the first round.

The first item follows by substituting
\beta_{0}=1
in
Eq.

9
.
Note that, since
\beta_{0}=1
, we have
1=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{0}(i)\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i\in[k]}1^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{0}(i)^{2}}=\sqrt{k\gamma_{0}}
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
so
\gamma_{0}\geq 1/k
.

We now prove the second item. From
Eq.

9
, we have

\displaystyle p_{\bot}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\beta_{0})^{(1-\beta_{0})n}\cdot\prod_{i\in[k]}\beta_{0}^{\alpha_{0}(i)n}

\displaystyle=(1-\beta_{0})^{(1-\beta_{0})n}\cdot\beta_{0}^{\beta_{0}n}

\displaystyle=2^{-n\mathrm{H}(\beta_{0})},

where
\mathrm{H}(x)=-x\log_{2}x-(1-x)\log_{2}(1-x)
denotes the binary entropy function.
Since
\frac{1}{n}\leq\beta_{0}\leq 1-\frac{1}{n}
, we have
\mathrm{H}(\beta_{0})\geq\mathrm{H}(1/n)\geq\frac{\log_{2}n}{n}
. This proves the second item.
∎

5.2
Behavior of the Fraction of Decided Vertices

From
Lemmas

5.1
and
C.1
, we know that the fraction of undecided vertices is
0
with probability
p_{\bot}
or otherwise lies between
1/n
and
\gamma_{0}\log n
with high probability.
In this subsection we show that
when the latter occurs, then it holds with high probability that
\beta_{t}\geq 1/2-o(1)
for all
\Omega(\log n)\leq t\leq n^{O(1)}
;
Lemma

5.2
.
In particular, this allows us to treat
\beta_{t}
as
\Omega(1)
for all such
t
.
See
Fig.

2
for an illustration.

In addition, we prove

Lemma

5.3
regarding the potential
\psi_{t}=\beta_{t}(2\beta_{t}-1)-\gamma_{t}
, which is related to a lower bound of
\beta_{t}
.
Note that by definition,
\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]=\beta_{t-1}-\psi_{t-1}
.

Figure 2:
Behavior of
\beta_{t}
over time in the gossip model.

Lemma 5.2

(Growth of
\beta_{t}
)
.

We have the following:

1.

Let
C>0
be an arbitrary constant.
Suppose that
\beta_{0}>0
. Then, for some
T=O(\log n)
,

\beta_{T}\geq\frac{C\log n}{n}
with probability at least
1-O\quantity(\frac{\log n}{n})
.

2.

For some
T=O(\log n)
,

\Pr\quantity[ \taubetaplus> T ]\leq T\quantity(\exp\qty(-\Omega(n \beta_0))+\exp\qty(-\Omega(n\xbeta^2))).

3.

Suppose that
\psi_{0}\leq x_{\psi}
and
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x_{\beta}
hold.
Then, for any
T\geq 1
,

\Pr\quantity[ \taubetaminus\le T \text{ and } \taupsiplus> T]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n \xbeta^2)).

Lemma 5.3

(Decay of
\psi_{t}
)
.

We have the following:

1.

\Pr[\tau^{-}_{\psi}>1]\leq\exp(-\Omega(n\xpsi^2))
for any initial configuration.

2.

Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x_{\psi}
. Then, for any
T\geq 1
,

\Pr[\tau_{\psi}^{+}\leq T]\leq T\exp(-\Omega(n\xpsi^2))
.

Proof of
Item

1
of
Lemma

5.2
.

First, for
\beta_{0}<C\log n/n\leq 1/4
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]

\displaystyle=2\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}\geq\frac{3}{2}\beta_{t-1}.

(10)

Note that we use
Lemma

4.3
(
Item

1
).
From
Lemma

4.3
(
Item

3
) and
Remark

3.6
,

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[ \beta_t \le\frac{5}{4}\beta_{t-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{t-1}\beta_{t}\leq\quantity[ \beta_t \le\E_{t-1}[\beta_t]-\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{4}]_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{4}\leq\exp\quantity(-\,\frac{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\beta_{t-1}^2}{16}}{\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{n}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{12n}})\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n\beta_{t-1})).

(11)

Let
\tau_{\mathrm{zero}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\beta_{t}=0\}
be the first time when
\beta_{t}
becomes
0
.
From
Lemma

5.1
, we have
\Pr\quantity[\tauzero\leq T]\leq T/n
.
Further, by assumption of
\beta_{0}>0
, we have
\tau_{\mathrm{zero}}>0
.
We apply
Lemma

A.6
for
Z_{t}=\mathrm{opn}_{t}
,
T=1
,
\varphi(Z_{t})=\sqrt{n\beta_{t}}
,
\tau=\tau_{\mathrm{zero}}
,
c_{\varphi}^{\uparrow}=\sqrt{5/4}-1
,
x_{0}=1
, and
x^{*}=\sqrt{C\log n}
. From these settings, we have

\tau^{+}_{\varphi}(x_{0})=\inf\{t\geq 0:\sqrt{n\beta_{t}}\geq 1\}=0
,

\tau^{+}_{\varphi}(x_{*})=\inf\{t\geq 0:\sqrt{n\beta_{t}}\geq\sqrt{C\log n/n}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\beta_{t}\geq C\log n/n\}
, and

\tau^{\uparrow}_{\varphi}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\sqrt{n\beta_{t}}\geq(1+c_{\varphi}^{\uparrow})\sqrt{n\beta_{0}}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\beta_{t}\geq(5/4)\beta_{0}\}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\tauphiplus(x_0), \tau}\leq 1]=1

holds, i.e., the first condition of
Lemma

A.6
is satisfied for
C_{1}=1
.
Next, for any configuration of
\beta_{0}\leq C\log n/n
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\tauphiup, \tau}>1]\geq\Pr\quantity[\beta_1\leq\frac{5}{4}\beta_0]\geq 1-\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n\beta_0))=1-\exp\quantity(-\Omega(\varphi(Z_0)^2)),

i.e., the second condition of
Lemma

A.6
is satisfied for some positive constant
C_{2}>0
.
Note that we use
Eq.

11
in the second inequality.

Thus, from
Lemma

A.6
with
\varepsilon=n^{-10}
,
for some
T^{\prime}=O(\log n)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauphiplus(x_*)>T']

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\tauphiplus(x_*), \tauzero}>T']+\Pr\quantity[\tauzero\leq T']

\displaystyle\leq n^{-10}+T^{\prime}/n.

∎

Proof of
Item

2
of
Lemma

5.2
.

First, consider the case where
\beta_{t-1}\leq\frac{1}{4}
.
Recall
Eq.

11
: For any
\beta_{t-1}\leq 1/4
,

\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[ \beta_t \le(5/4)\beta_{t-1}]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n\beta_{t-1}))
.
Therefore, for some
T=O(\log n)
rounds,

\beta_{T}\geq\frac{1}{4}
with probability at least
1-T\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n \beta_0))
.

Second, consider the case where
\frac{1}{4}\leq\beta_{t-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}-x_{\beta}
.
Write
B_{t}=1-2\beta_{t}
for convenience.
Recall that
\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]=2\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}
.
Since
2x_{\beta}\leq B_{t-1}\leq 1/2
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[B_{t}]

\displaystyle=1-4\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1})-2\gamma_{t-1}\leq B_{t-1}^{2}\leq\frac{B_{t-1}}{2}.

Furthermore, from
B_{t}-\E_{t-1}[B_{t}]=2(\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\beta_{t})
,
Lemma

3.7
(
Item

3
), and
Lemma

4.3
(
Item

3
),

B_{t}-\E_{t-1}[B_{t}]
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{2}{n}, \frac{4\beta_{t-1}}{n})
-Bernstein condition.
Hence,

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[B_t\geq\frac{3}{4}B_{t-1}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{t-1}B_{t}\geq\quantity[B_t\geq\E_{t-1}[B_t]+\frac{B_{t-1}}{2}]_{t-1}[B_{t}]+\frac{B_{t-1}}{2}\leq\exp(-\Omega(nB_{t-1}^2)).

Therefore, for some
T^{\prime}=O(\log n)
rounds,
B_{T^{\prime}}\leq 2x_{\beta}
holds with a probability at least
1-T_{1}\exp(-\Omega(n\xbeta^2))
.
Note that
B_{t}\leq 2x_{\beta}
implies
\beta_{t}\geq 1/2-x_{\beta}
.
∎

Proof of
Item

3
of
Lemma

5.2
.

First, consider the case where
\frac{1}{2}-x_{\beta}\leq\beta_{t-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{x_{\beta}}{2}
.
Note that
\frac{1}{4}\leq\frac{1}{2}-x_{\beta}
.
From
Lemma

4.3
(
Item

1
),

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\beta_{t-1}\geq\beta_{t-1}(1-2\beta_{t-1})\geq\quantity(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\xbeta}{2})x_{\beta}\geq\frac{x_{\beta}}{4}.

Hence,
from
Lemma

4.3
(
Item

3
),

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\beta_t \leq\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\beta_{t}\leq\beta_{t-1}]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\beta_{t}\leq\quantity[\beta_{t}\leq\E_{t-1}[\beta_t]-\frac{\xbeta}{4}]_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\frac{x_{\beta}}{4}\leq\exp(-\Omega(n\xbeta^2)).

Second, consider the case where
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{x_{\beta}}{2}\leq\beta_{t-1}
.
Recall
x_{\beta}\geq 4x_{\psi}
.
Then, from
Lemma

4.3
(
Items

1
and
3
), for
\tau_{\psi}^{+}>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\beta_t \leq\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\beta_{t}\leq\beta_{t-1}-\psi_{t-1}-\frac{\xbeta}{4}]=\Pr_{t-1}\beta_{t}\leq\quantity[\beta_{t}\leq\E_{t-1}[\beta_t]-\frac{\xbeta}{4}]_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\frac{x_{\beta}}{4}\leq\exp(-\Omega(n\xbeta^2)).

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taubetaminus\leq T \text{ and } \taupsiplus>T]

\displaystyle=\Pr\quantity[\exists t\leq T: \beta_t\leq\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta\text{ and }\taupsiplus>T]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\taupsiplus>t-1}\Pr_{t-1}\qty[\beta_t \leq\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta]]

\displaystyle\leq T\exp(-\Omega(n\xbeta^2)).

Note that
\Pr[\beta_{t}\leq\frac{1}{2}-x_{\beta}\text{ and }\tau_{\psi}^{+}>t-1]=\E\quantity[\indicator_{\taupsiplus>t-1}\Pr_{t-1}\qty[\beta_t \leq\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta]]
.
∎

Proof of
Item

1
of
Lemma

5.3
.

From
Lemma

4.6
(
Item

1
),

\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]+\frac{x_{\psi}}{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}+\frac{x_{\psi}}{2}\leq x_{\psi}
holds.
Further, from
Lemma

4.6
(
Item

2
),
\psi_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]
satisfies
\quantity(O(1/n),O(1/n))
-Bernstein condition.
Hence, from
Remark

3.6
,

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\psi_t> \xpsi]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\psi_{t}>\quantity[\psi_t> \E_{t-1}[\psi_t]+\frac{\xpsi}{2}]_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]+\frac{x_{\psi}}{2}\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\xpsi^2}{\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\xpsi}{n}}))\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\xpsi^2)).

(12)

We obtain the claim from
\Pr\quantity[\taupsiminus>1]\leq\Pr\quantity[\psi_1> \xpsi]
.
∎

Proof of
Item

2
of
Lemma

5.3
.

From the union bound and
Eq.

12
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tau_{\psi}^{+}\leq T]=\Pr[\bigvee_{t=1}^{T}\quantity{\psi_t> \xpsi}]\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Pr[\psi_{t}>x_{\psi}]=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\E\quantity[\Pr_{t-1}\qty[\psi_t>\xpsi]]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n\xpsi^2)).

Note that
\tau_{\psi}^{+}\neq 0
since
\psi_{0}\leq x_{\psi}
.
∎

5.3
Behavior of Squared
\ell^{2}
Norm of Normalized Population

In this subsection, we track the change of the quantity
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=\norm{\alpha_t}_{2}^{2}/\beta_{t}^{2}
defined in
Definition

4.1
.
We show that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
becomes
\Omega((\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
within
\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})
rounds with high probability.
Recall the definition of
x_{\gamma}=(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n}
in
Definition

4.16
.

Lemma 5.4

(Growth of
\gamma_{t}
)
.

Suppose
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x_{\beta}
and
\psi_{0}\leq x_{\psi}
.
Then, for some
T=O(nx_{\gamma}\log n)
,

\Pr\quantity[\taugammaplus>T \text{ or } \min\{\taubetaminus,\taupsiplus\}\leq T]\leq n^{-\Omega(1)}.

The intuition behind
Lemma

5.4
is as follows.
First, we derive an upper bound on the expected value of
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-},\tau_{\psi}^{+}\}
in terms of
\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]
.
The key technique is to combine the additive drift of
\widetilde{\gamma}
with the optional stopping theorem (
Theorem

A.4
).
Second, to obtain an upper bound on
\E[\tau]
, we provide an upper bound of
\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]
.

Lemma 5.5
.

\E\quantity[\min\{\taugammaplus,\taubetaminus,\taupsiplus\}]\leq 1320nx_{\gamma}.

Proof.

For
\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-},\tau_{\psi}^{+}\}>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_t]

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{12n}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.8
.
Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-},\tau_{\psi}^{+}\}
,
X_{t}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}-\frac{t}{12}
and
Y_{t}=X_{t\wedge\tau}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[Y_t-Y_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\E_{t-1}\quantity[X_t-X_{t-1}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\tnpt_t]-\frac{t}{12}-\tnpt_{t-1}+\frac{t-1}{12})_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]-\frac{t}{12}-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{t-1}{12}\geq 0,

i.e.,
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a submartingale.
Hence, from
Theorem

A.4
, we have
\E[Y_{\tau}]\geq\E[Y_{0}]=\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\geq 0
.
Thus,

\displaystyle 0\leq\E[Y_{\tau}]=\E[X_{\tau}]=\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]-\frac{\E[\tau]}{12n}

and we obtain
\E[\tau]\leq 12n\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]
.

Now, we give an upper bound on
\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]
.
Write
L=55x_{\gamma}
.
We have

\displaystyle\E\quantity[\tnpt_\tau]

\displaystyle=\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpt_\tau\leq L}\tnpt_\tau]+\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpt_\tau> L}\tnpt_\tau]\leq L+\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpt_\tau> L}\tnpt_\tau]

and

\displaystyle\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpt_\tau> L}\tnpt_\tau]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau=t}\tnpt_t\indicator_{\tnpt_t> L}]\leq\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau>t-1}\tnpt_t\indicator_{\tnpt_t> L}]=\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\E\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity[\indicator_{\tau>t-1}\E_{t-1}\qty[\tnpt_t\indicator_{\tnpt_t> L}]]_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_t\indicator_{\tnpt_t> L}].

For
t-1<\tau
, we have
\beta_{t-1}\geq 1/2-x_{\beta}
and
\gamma_{t-1}\leq x_{\gamma}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_\tau\indicator_{\tnpt_\tau> L}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_t> L]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\gamma_t> \qty(\frac{1}{2}-\xbeta)^2L]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\quantity[\gamma_t> 11\xgamma]\leq\Pr_{t-1}\gamma_{t}>\quantity[\gamma_t> \E_{t-1}[\gamma_t]+\xgamma]_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]+x_{\gamma}.

Note that
\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]\leq 10\gamma_{t-1}
.
Since
\gamma_{t}-\E_{t-1}[\gamma_{t}]
satisfies
