We split the proof into two phases:
0<\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-c
and
\frac{1}{2}-c\leq\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-x
.

Phase 1.
0<\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-c
.

Thus, applying
Eq.

27
for some
\ell=O(\log n)
times,
\beta_{t}
gets greater than or equal to
1/2-c
within
\ell n=O(n\log n)
steps with probability at least
1-\ell\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\beta_0))
.

Case 2.
\frac{1}{2}-c\leq\beta_{0}<\frac{1}{2}-x
.

In this case, consider the parameter
B_{t}=1-2\beta_{t}
(note that
\beta_{t}=(1-B_{t})/2
and
2x\leq B_{0}\leq 2c
).
For positive constants
c_{B}^{\uparrow},c_{B}^{\downarrow}\in(0,1)
, let
\tau_{B}^{\uparrow}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:B_{t}\geq(1+c_{B}^{\uparrow})B_{0}\}
,
\tau_{B}^{\downarrow}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:B_{t}\leq(1-c_{B}^{\downarrow})B_{0}\}
,

\tau^{2x}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:B_{t}\leq 2x\}=\tau_{\beta}^{+}(x)
,
and
\tau=\min\{\tau_{B}^{\uparrow},\tau_{B}^{\downarrow},\tau^{2x}\}
.
For
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[B_{t}]=1-2\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]=B_{t-1}-\frac{B_{t-1}(1-B_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}}{n}\leq B_{t-1}-\frac{(1-c_{B}^{\downarrow})(1-(1+c_{B}^{\downarrow})B_{0})B_{0}}{n}.

(28)

We use
Lemma

4.10
(
Item

1
) in the first equality.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=-B_{t}
and
R=\frac{(1-c_{B}^{\downarrow})(1-(1+c_{B}^{\downarrow})B_{0})B_{0}}{n}\geq 0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[B_t]-B_{t-1}+\drift)_{t-1}[B_{t}]-B_{t-1}+R\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=2\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\beta_t]-\beta_t)_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\beta_{t}
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{2}{n},\frac{4}{n^2})
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.10
(
Item

3
),
\beta_{t-1}\leq 1
, and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) with
I^{+}=X_{0}+c^{\downarrow}_{B}B_{0}
,
I^{-}=X_{0}-c^{\uparrow}_{B}B_{0}
, and
T^{\prime}=\frac{(1+\varepsilon)c_{B}^{\downarrow}}{(1-c_{B}^{\downarrow})(1-(1+c_{B}^{\downarrow})B_{0})}n=\Theta(n)
for an arbitrary constant
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_B^\downarrow,\tau_\beta^+(x) \} >T']=\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_B^\downarrow,\tau^{2x} \} >T']\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nB_0^2))\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

(29)

Thus, applying
Eq.

29
for some
\ell=O(\log n)
times,
B_{t}
gets less than or equal to
2x
, i.e.,
\beta_{t}\geq 1/2-x
within
T=O(n\log n)
steps with probability at least
1-\ell\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2))
.
∎

Proof of
Item

3
of
Lemma

6.1
.

Let
I^{-}=1/2-2x
,
I_{*}^{-}=1/2-x
,
I_{*}^{+}=1/2-x/2
and
I^{+}=1/2-x/4
.
Note that
I^{+}-I^{+}_{*}=I^{+}_{*}-I^{-}_{*}=I^{-}_{*}-I^{-}=\Theta(x)
.
Let
\tau^{+}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\beta_{t}\geq I_{*}^{+}\}
and
\tau=\min\{\tau^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x)\}
.
Then, for
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]

\displaystyle=\beta_{t-1}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}\quantity(1-2\beta_{t-1})+\gamma_{t-1}}{n}\geq\beta_{t-1}+\frac{x}{6n}.

(30)

We use
Lemma

4.10
in the first equality.
Hence, letting
X_{t}=\beta_{t}
and
R=\frac{x}{6n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\beta_{t-1}+\frac{x}{6n}-\quantity(\beta_{t-1}+\frac{x}{6n}-\E_{t-1}[\beta_t])_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\beta_t]-\beta_t)_{t-1}[\beta_{t}]-\beta_{t}
satisfies
\quantity(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n^2})
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.10
(
Item

3
),
\beta_{t-1}\leq 1
, and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) for
\beta_{0}\in[I_{*}^{+},I^{+}]
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau_\beta^-(2x)<\tau^+]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

(31)

Consequently, from
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

2
) with
\tau_{s}^{-}=\inf\{t\geq s:\beta_{t}\leq I^{-}\}
and
\tau_{s}^{+}=\inf\{t\geq s:\beta_{t}\geq I^{+}\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^-_\beta(2x)\leq T]\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\beta_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*]}\Pr_s\qty[\tau^-_s<\tau^+_s]]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n x^2)).

∎

Proof of
Item

1
of
Lemma

6.2
.

Let
c_{\psi}^{\uparrow},c_{\psi}^{\downarrow}\in(0,1)
be constants,

\tau_{\psi}^{\uparrow}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\psi_{t}\geq(1+c_{\psi}^{\uparrow})\psi_{0}\}
, and

\tau_{\psi}^{\downarrow}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\psi_{t}\leq(1-c_{\psi}^{\downarrow})\psi_{0}\}
.
Write
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\psi}^{\uparrow},\tau_{\psi}^{\downarrow},\tau_{\psi}^{-}(x)\}
.

For any
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{t-1}-\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{n}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}}{n^{2}}\leq\psi_{t-1}-\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{n}+\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{2n}\leq\psi_{t-1}-\frac{(1-c_{\psi}^{\downarrow})\psi_{0}}{2n}.

(32)

We use
Lemma

4.13
(
Item

1
) in the first equality.
Note that
\psi_{t-1}\geq x\geq\frac{2}{n}
.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=-\psi_{t}
and
R=\frac{(1-c_{\psi}^{\downarrow})\psi_{0}}{2n}\geq 0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\psi_t]-\psi_{t-1}+\frac{(1-c_\psi^\downarrow)\psi_{0}}{2n})_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]-\psi_{t-1}+\frac{(1-c_{\psi}^{\downarrow})\psi_{0}}{2n}\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\psi_{t}-\quantity(\psi_t-\E_{t-1}[\psi_t])_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]
satisfies
\quantity(O(1/n),O(1/n^2))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.13
(
Item

2
),
\beta_{t-1}\leq 1
, and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) with
I^{+}=X_{0}+c^{\downarrow}_{\psi}\psi_{0}
,
I^{-}=X_{0}-c^{\uparrow}_{\psi}\psi_{0}
, and
T^{\prime}=\frac{2(1+\varepsilon)c_{\psi}^{\downarrow}}{1-c_{\psi}^{\downarrow}}n=\Theta(n)
for an arbitrary constant
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_\psi^\downarrow,\tau_\psi^- \} >T']\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\psi_0^2))\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

(33)

Thus, applying
Eq.

33
for some
\ell=O(\log n)
times repeatedly,
\psi_{t}
gets less than or equal to
x
within
\ell n=O(n\log n)
steps with probability at least
1-\ell\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2))
.
∎

Proof of
Item

2
of
Lemma

6.2
.

Let
\tau^{x/4}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\psi_{t}\leq x/4\}
.
Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\psi}^{+}(2x),\tau^{x/4}\}
.
Then, for
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]

\displaystyle=\psi_{t-1}-\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{n}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}}{n^{2}}\leq\psi_{t-1}-\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{n}+\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{2n}\leq\psi_{t-1}-\frac{x}{8n}.

(34)

We use
Lemma

4.13
in the first equality.
Note that
\psi_{t-1}\geq x/4\geq\frac{2}{n}
.
Hence, letting
X_{t}=-\psi_{t}
and
R=\frac{x}{8n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\psi_t]-\psi_{t-1}+\frac{x}{8n})_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]-\psi_{t-1}+\frac{x}{8n}\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\psi_{t}-\quantity(\psi_t-\E_{t-1}[\psi_t])_{t-1}[\psi_{t}]
satisfies
\quantity(O(1/n),O(1/n^2))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.13
(
Item

2
),
\beta_{t-1}\leq 1
, and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Let
I^{-}=-2x
,
I^{-}_{*}=-x
,
I^{+}_{*}=-x/2
, and
I^{+}=-x/4
.
Let
\tau^{+}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq I^{+}\}=\tau^{x/4}
and
\tau^{-}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq I^{-}\}=\tau_{\psi}^{+}(2x)
.
Note that
I^{+}-I^{+}_{*}=I^{+}_{*}-I^{-}_{*}=I^{-}_{*}-I^{-}=\Theta(x)
.
Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) for
X_{0}\in[I_{*}^{-},I^{+}_{*}]
, i.e.,
\psi_{0}\in[x/2,x]
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tau^{-}<\tau^{+}]=\Pr\quantity[\taupsiplus(2x)<\tau^-]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

(35)

Consequently, applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

2
) with
\tau_{s}^{-}=\inf\{t\geq s:X_{t}\leq I^{-}\}=\inf\{t\geq s:\psi_{t}\geq 2x\}
and
\tau_{s}^{+}=\inf\{t\geq s:X_{t}\geq I^{+}\}=\inf\{t\geq s:\psi_{t}\leq x/4\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taupsiplus(2x)\leq T]\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\psi_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*]}\Pr_s\qty[\tau^-_s<\tau^+_s]]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

∎

6.2
Behavior of Squared
\ell^{2}
Norm of Normalized Population

In this section, we show that
\gamma_{t}
reaches
x_{\gamma}
within
O(n^{2}x_{\gamma}\log n)
steps with high probability (
Lemma

6.3
).
Recall
\tau_{\gamma}^{+}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\gamma_{t}\geq x_{\gamma}\}
for some
x_{\gamma}=(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n}
(see
Definition

4.16
).
The main difference from the gossip model is the magnitude of the drift of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
:
In the gossip model, we have
\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\Omega(1/n)
, whereas in the population protocol model,
\E_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\Omega(1/n^{2})
holds.

Lemma 6.3

(Growth of
\gamma_{t}
)
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
.
Then, for some
T=O(n^{2}x_{\gamma}\log n)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taugammaplus>T \text{ or } \taubetaminus(2x)\leq T]\leq n^{-10}.

The key idea of the proof of
Lemma

6.3
is to combine the additive drift of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
(
Lemma

4.15
) and the optional stopping theorem.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
.
Then,
\E\quantity[\min\{\taugammaplus,\taubetaminus(x)\}]\leq 72n^{2}x_{\gamma}
.

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.15
,
for
\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x)\}>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpt_t]

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{1}{12n^{2}}.

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x)\}
,
X_{t}=\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}-\frac{t}{12n^{2}}
and
Y_{t}=X_{t\wedge\tau}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[Y_t-Y_{t-1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\E_{t-1}\quantity[X_t-X_{t-1}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\tnpt_t]-\frac{t}{12n^2}-\tnpt_{t-1}+\frac{t-1}{12n^2})_{t-1}[\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}]-\frac{t}{12n^{2}}-\widetilde{\gamma}_{t-1}+\frac{t-1}{12n^{2}}\geq 0,

i.e.,
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a submartingale.
Hence, from
Theorem

A.4
, we have
\E[Y_{\tau}]\geq\E[Y_{0}]=\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\geq 0
.
Thus,

0\leq\E[Y_{\tau}]=\E[X_{\tau}]=\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]-\frac{\E[\tau]}{12n^{2}}

and we obtain

\displaystyle\E[\tau]\leq 12n^{2}\E[\widetilde{\gamma}_{\tau}]\leq 36n^{2}(x_{\gamma}+1)\leq 72n^{2}x_{\gamma}.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.3
.

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\gamma}^{+},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x)\}
and
T^{\prime}=72\mathrm{e}n^{2}x_{\gamma}
.
From the Markov inequality and
Lemma

6.4
, if
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-2x
for some
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(1)
(i.e.,
2x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
2x=o(1)
),

\Pr\quantity[\tau>T']\leq\frac{\E[\tau]}{T^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}}.

Hence, for any
\ell\geq 1
, the Markov property implies that

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau>\ell T' \mid\tau> (\ell-1) T']

\displaystyle=\E\quantity[\Pr_{(\ell-1)T'}\qty[\tau>\ell T'] \mid\tau> (\ell-1) T']\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau>\ell T']

\displaystyle=\Pr\quantity[\tau>\ell T' \mid\tau> (\ell-1) T']\Pr\quantity[\tau> (\ell-1) T']\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}}\Pr\quantity[\tau> (\ell-1) T']\leq\cdots\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\ell}}.

Applying
Lemma

6.1
(
Item

3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taugammaplus> \ell T' \text{ or } \taubetaminus(2x) \leq\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\taugammaplus> \ell T' \text{ or } \taubetaminus(2x) \leq\ell T'} \text{ and } \taubetaminus(2x) > \ell T']+\Pr\quantity[\taubetaminus(2x)\leq\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{\ell}}+\ell T^{\prime}\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

Taking
\ell=C\log n
for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
, we obtain the claim.
∎

6.3
Behavior of Maximum Population

In this section, we show that
(i)
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
holds for all
t\leq O(n^{2}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}/\log n)
with high probability (
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

1
)) and
(ii)
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\leq(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
holds for all
t\leq O(n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
with high probability (
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

2
)).
Intuitively, in the population protocol model, both the drift and the variance
s
in the Bernstein condition for
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
(or
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
) are roughly a factor of
1/n
smaller compared to those in the gossip model.

Lemma 6.5

(Key properties of
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
)
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
.
We have the following:

1.

Let
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}=c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}/72
be a positive constant.
Then, for any
T\leq C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
,

\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxdown\leq T \text{ and } \taubetaminus(x)> T]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\tnpm_0n^2}{T+n})).

2.

Suppose
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Let
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}=\frac{c_{\max}^{\uparrow}}{6(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{2}}
.
Then, for any
T\leq\frac{C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}n}{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxup\leq\min\qty{T,\taubetaminus(x)}]\leq k\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\npm_0 n^2}{T+n})).

The key tool for the proof of
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

1
) is the following lemma.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
.
Let
\tau^{\uparrow}=\max\{\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow},\tau^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}\}
.
Let
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}=c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}/72
be a positive constant defined in
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

1
).
Then, for any
T\leq C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxdown\leq\min\{T,\tau^\uparrow,\taubetaminus(x)\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \Omega\qty(\frac{\tnpm_0 n^2}{T+n})).

Proof.

Let
\tau^{\uparrow}=\max\{\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow},\tau^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}\}
and
\tau=\min\{\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau^{\uparrow},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x)\}
.
From
Lemma

4.14
(
Item

1
),
for
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpm_t]

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\frac{18\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n^{2}}\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}-\frac{36\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n^{2}}.

Note that

\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\leq(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\leq 2\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}

or
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\leq(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\beta_{t-1}\leq 2\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
, i.e.,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\leq 2\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
holds for
\tau^{\uparrow}>t-1
.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
R=-\frac{36\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n^{2}}<0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}-\frac{36\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n^{2}}-\quantity(\tnpm_{t-1}-\frac{36\tnpm_{0}}{n^2}-\E_{t-1}[\tnpm_t])_{t-1}[\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}]\leq 0.

Furthermore,

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\tnpm_{t-1}\qty(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\frac{18\npm_{t-1}}{n^2}-\tnpm_t)

satisfies one-sided
\quantity(O\qty(\frac{1}{n}),O\qty(\frac{\npm_0}{n^2}))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.14
(
Item

2
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

1
) with
I^{-}=(1+c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
,
I^{+}=(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
, and
T\leq\frac{X_{0}-I^{-}}{-2R}=\frac{c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}{72}n^{2}=C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxdown\leq\min\{T,\tau^\uparrow,\taubetaminus(x)\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \Omega\qty(\frac{\tnpm_0 n^2}{T+n})).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

1
).

For
s\geq 0
, let
\tau_{s}^{\downarrow}=\inf\{t\geq s:\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\leq(1+c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{s}\}
and
\tau_{s}^{\uparrow}=\inf\{t\geq s:\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{s}\}
.
Applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

1
) with
T\leq C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxdown\leq T \text{ and } \taubetaminus(x)> T]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpm_s\geq\tnpm_0 \text{ and } \taubetaminus(x)> s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau_s^\downarrow\leq\min\{T,\tau_s^\uparrow,\taubetaminus(x)\}]]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tnpm_s\geq\tnpm_0 \text{ and } \taubetaminus(x)> s}\exp\qty(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\tnpm_sn^2}{T+n}))]

\displaystyle\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\tnpm_0n^2}{T+n})).

Note that we apply
Lemma

6.6
in the second inequality.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

2
).

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x)\}
.
For
\tau>t-1
, similarly to
Eq.

13
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]-\alpha_{t-1}(i)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t-1}(i)\cdot\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+1-2\beta_{t-1}}{n}\leq\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\frac{3(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{2}(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})^{2}}{n}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.9
(
Item

1
).
Hence, letting
X_{t}=-\alpha_{t}(i)
and
R=-\frac{3(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{2}(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})^{2}}{n}<0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(i)]-\alpha_{t-1}(i)-\frac{3(1+\cmaxup)^2(\npm_{0})^2}{n})_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]-\alpha_{t-1}(i)-\frac{3(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{2}(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})^{2}}{n}\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\alpha_{t}(i)-\quantity(\alpha_t(i)-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(i)])_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]
satisfies
\quantity(1/n,O(\npm_0/n^2))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.9
(
Item

3
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Let
\tau_{i}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\alpha_{t}(i)\geq(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq-(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}
.
Recall
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}=\frac{c_{\max}^{\uparrow}}{6(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{2}}
.
Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

1
) with

I^{-}=-(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
, for
T\leq\frac{C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}n}{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}=\frac{X_{0}-I^{-}}{-2R}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau_i\leq\min\{T,\tau\}]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\npm_0 n^2}{T+n})).

Thus, applying the union bound,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxup\leq\min\{T,\tau\}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\exists i\in[k]: \tau_i\leq\min\{T,\tau\}]\leq k\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{\npm_0 n^2}{T+n})).

∎

The following lemma follows naturally from the preceding discussion.
Note that the bounds concerning to
\beta_{t}
and
\psi_{t}
are different from those for the gossip model.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose that
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
and
\psi_{0}\leq x
. Then, for any
T\leq C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\} \leq T]\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{n^2\tnpm_0}{T+n}))+T\exp\quantity(-\Omega(nx^2)).

Proof.

Combining
Eqs.

14
and
6.5
(
Item

1
),
Lemma

6.1
,
Lemma

6.2
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\} \leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxdown\leq T \text{ and }\min\{\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T]+\Pr\quantity[\taubetaminus(2x)\leq T]+\Pr\quantity[\taupsiplus(2x)\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{n^2\tnpm_0}{T+n}))+T\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nx^2)).

∎

6.4
Behavior of Gap between Two Opinions

Recall the definition of the weak opinion and its stopping time in
Definition

5.9
.
The main results of this section is the following lemma.

Lemma 6.8

(Either of two non-weak opinions becomes weak)
.

Let
i,j\in[k]
be an arbitrary pair of two non-weak opinions.
Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
We have the following:

1.

Let
C
be an arbitrary positive constant.
Then, for some
T=O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\taudeltaplus\qty(\sqrt{\frac{C\log n}{n}}),\tauiweak,\taujweak} > T \text{ or } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\} \leq T]\leq n^{-10}.

2.

Suppose
\delta_{0}(i,j)\geq C\sqrt{\log n/n}
for a sufficiently large constant
C>0
.
Then, for some
T=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[ \taujweak>T \text{ or } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\} \leq T ]\leq n^{-10}.

In the case of the population protocol model, both the drift and the variance
s
in the Bernstein condition for
\delta_{t}
become smaller by about a factor of
1/n
compared to the gossip model.
We show the following lemma.

Lemma 6.9

(Multiplicative and additive drifts of
\delta_{t}
)
.

Let
i,j\in[k]
be an arbitrary pair of two non-weak opinions.
Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose that
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\psi_{0}\leq x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
We have the following:

1.

Suppose
\delta_{0}(i,j)\geq 0
.
Let
c_{\delta}^{\uparrow}=C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}\frac{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})(1-c_{\delta}^{\downarrow})\quantity(1-2\cweak)}{12}
, where
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}
is a positive constant defined in
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

2
).
Then,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_\delta^\uparrow,\taujweak,\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\} >\frac{\constrefs{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}{item:taualphamaxup for PP} n}{\npm_0}]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\delta_0(i,j)^2))+n\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n(\npm_0)^2)).

2.

Let
x_{\delta}=c/\sqrt{n}
, where
c\in(0,1)
is an arbitrary constant.
Let
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:delta multiplicative and additive drift for PP}(\ref{item:taudeltaplus const prob for population protocol model})}}=\frac{24c}{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\taudeltaplus,\tauiweak,\taujweak,\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(x),\taupsiplus(x)} >\frac{\constrefs{lem:delta multiplicative and additive drift for PP}{item:taudeltaplus const prob for population protocol model} n}{\npm_0}]\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Proof of
Lemma

6.9
(
Item

1
).

Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau_{j}^{\mathrm{weak}},\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow},\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(2x)\}
and
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow},\tau_{\delta}^{\downarrow},\tau^{*}\}
.
For
\tau>t-1
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{t-1}+\frac{\delta_{t-1}\beta_{t-1}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n}\quantity(\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)+\alpha_{t-1}(j)}{\npm_{t-1}}-\frac{\gamma_{t-1}}{\beta_{t-1}\npm_{t-1}}-\frac{\psi_{t-1}}{\beta_{t-1}^2\tnpm_{t-1}})

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{t-1}+\frac{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})(1-c_{\delta}^{\downarrow})\quantity(1-2\cweak)}{6}\cdot\frac{\delta_{0}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.11
(
Item

1
) and
Eq.

15
.

Letting
X_{t}=\delta_{t}
and
R=\frac{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})(1-c_{\delta}^{\downarrow})\quantity(1-2\cweak)}{6}\cdot\frac{\delta_{0}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\delta_{t-1}+R-\quantity(\delta_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[\delta_t])_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\delta_t]-\delta_t)_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]-\delta_{t}
satisfies
\quantity(O(1/n),O(\npm_0/n^2))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.11
(
Item

3
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Let
I^{+}=(1+c)\delta_{0}
and
I^{-}=(1-c)\delta_{0}
.
Recall that
c_{\delta}^{\uparrow}=C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}\frac{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})(1-c_{\delta}^{\downarrow})\quantity(1-2\cweak)}{12}
. Let
T=\frac{C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}n}{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}
.
Then, we have
T=\frac{2(I^{+}-\delta_{0})}{R}
.
Thus, we can apply
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) and obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_\delta^\uparrow,\tau^*\} >T]\ \leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\delta_0^2)).

Applying
Lemma

6.5
(
Item

2
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taudeltaup,\taujweak,\tautildemaxdown,\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T \text{ and } \taubetaminus(2x)>T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taudeltaup,\taujweak,\tautildemaxdown,\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T \text{ and } \taubetaminus(2x)>T \text{ and } \taumaxup>T]

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\taumaxup\leq T \text{ and } \taubetaminus(2x)>T]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\delta_0^2))+n\exp\quantity(-\Omega(n(\npm_0)^2)).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.9
(
Item

2
).

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\delta}^{+},\tau_{i}^{\mathrm{weak}},\tau_{j}^{\mathrm{weak}},\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x)\}
.
For
t-1<\tau
,

\displaystyle\Var_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(i))}{n^{2}}+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(j)\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(j))}{n^{2}}-\frac{4\delta_{t-1}(i,j)^{2}}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:basic inequalities for delta PP}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}-4c^{2}/n}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle(\text{by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:weak npm upper bound}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}-12c^{2}/n}{3n^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{6n^{2}}.

Note that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(1/n)
.
Hence, for
t-1<\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{2}]=\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]^{2}+\Var_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]\geq\delta_{t-1}^{2}+\frac{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{6n^{2}}.

Note that
\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}]^{2}\geq\delta_{t-1}^{2}
holds for
\tau>t-1
(
Eq.

18
).
Let

R=\frac{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{6n^{2}}
and

X_{t}=\delta_{t}^{2}-Rt
and
Y_{t}=X_{t\wedge\tau}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[Y_{t}-Y_{t-1}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\E_{t-1}[X_{t}-X_{t-1}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\delta_t^2]-\drift t-\delta_{t-1}^2+\drift(t-1))_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{2}]-Rt-\delta_{t-1}^{2}+R(t-1)\geq 0,

i.e.,
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a submartingale.
From
Theorem

A.4
, we have

\E[Y_{\tau}]\geq\E[Y_{0}]=\delta_{0}^{2}\geq 0

and

\E[Y_{\tau}]=\E[X_{\tau}]=\E[\delta_{\tau}^{2}]-R\E[\tau]

hold.
Thus,

\displaystyle\E[\tau]\leq\frac{\E[\delta_{\tau}^{2}]}{R}\leq\frac{\quantity(\frac{c}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{n})^{2}}{R}\leq\frac{24c}{(1-2c_{\mathrm{weak}})(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})}\frac{n}{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}=\frac{C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:delta multiplicative and additive drift for PP}(\ref{item:taudeltaplus const prob for population protocol model})}}n}{2\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}.

We obtain the claim from the Markov inequality.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.8
(
Item

1
).

We apply
Lemma

A.6
for
Z_{t}=\mathrm{opn}_{t}
,
\varphi(Z_{t})=\sqrt{n}\cdot\absolutevalue{\delta_t(i,j)}
,

\tau=\min\quantity{\tauiweak,\taujweak,\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)},

c_{\varphi}^{\uparrow}=c_{\delta}^{\uparrow}
,
x_{0}=c_{\delta}^{+}
, and
x^{*}=\sqrt{C\log n}
.
Define

\displaystyle\tau^{\uparrow}=\begin{cases}\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(i,j)&\text{if }\delta_{0}(i,j)\geq 0,\\
\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(j,i)&\text{if }\delta_{0}(j,i)>0\;(\text{i.e., }\delta_{0}(i,j)<0)\end{cases},

where
\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow}(i,j)=\tau_{\delta}^{\uparrow}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\delta_{t}(i,j)\geq(1+c_{\delta}^{\uparrow})\delta_{0}(i,j)\}
.
Then, from these settings, we have

\displaystyle\tau^{+}_{\varphi}(x_{0})=\inf\{t\geq 0:\sqrt{n}\absolutevalue{\delta_t}\geq c_{\delta}^{+}\}=\tau_{\delta}^{+},

\displaystyle\tau^{\uparrow}_{\varphi}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\sqrt{n}\absolutevalue{\delta_t}\geq(1+c_{\delta}^{\uparrow})\sqrt{n}\absolutevalue{\delta_0}\}\leq\tau^{\uparrow}.

From
Lemma

6.9
(
Item

2
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\tauphiplus(x_0),\tau} > \frac{\constrefs{lem:delta multiplicative and additive drift for PP}{item:taudeltaplus const prob for population protocol model} n}{\npm_0}]\leq 1/2,

i.e., the first condition of
Lemma

A.6
holds for
C_{1}=1/2
.
Note that if
\beta_{0}<1/2-2x
or
\psi_{0}>2x
or
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\leq O(\sqrt{\log n/n})
, then
\tau=0
.
Next, from
Lemma

6.9
(
Item

1
),

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tauphiup,\tau\}> \frac{\constrefs{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}{item:taualphamaxup for PP} n}{\npm_0}]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^\uparrow,\tau\}> \frac{\constrefs{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}{item:taualphamaxup for PP} n}{\npm_0}]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\delta_0(i,j)^2))+n\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n(\npm_0)^2))

\displaystyle\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\varphi(Z_0)^2))

holds for
\delta_{0}(i,j)\leq C\sqrt{\log n/n}
, i.e., the second condition of
Lemma

A.6
holds for some positive constant
C_{2}
.
Note that
n\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n(\npm_0)^2))\leq n^{-\omega(1)}\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n\delta_0(i,j)^2))
from the assumption on
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
.
Note that if
\beta_{0}<1/2-2x
or
\psi_{0}>2x
or
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\leq O(\sqrt{\log n/n})
, then
\tau=0
.

Write
\tau^{\mathrm{weak}}=\min\{\tau_{i}^{\mathrm{weak}},\tau_{j}^{\mathrm{weak}}\}
and
\tau_{ini}=\min\{\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(2x)\}
.
From
Lemmas

A.6
and
6.7
with
\varepsilon=n^{-30}
, for some
T^{\prime}=O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taudeltaplus(\sqrt{C\log n/n}),\tauweak\}> T' \text{ or } \tau_{ini}\leq T']

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\min\{\tauphiplus(x_*),\tauweak\}> T' \text{ or } \tau_{ini}\leq T'}\text{ and } \tau_{ini}>T']+\Pr\quantity[\tau_{ini}\leq T']

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tauphiplus(x_*),\tauweak,\tau_{ini}\}> T']+\Pr\quantity[\tau_{ini}\leq T']

\displaystyle\leq n^{-20}.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.8
(
Item

2
).

Let
\tau^{\uparrow(s)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\delta_{t}\geq(1+c)^{s}\delta_{0}\}
.
Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(2x)\}
.
From definition, for some
\ell=\Theta(\log n)
,
\tau_{j}^{\mathrm{weak}}\leq\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)}
holds.
Let
T^{\prime}=\frac{3C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:taualphamaxup for PP})}}n}{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}
.
Applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

3
), we have
\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taujweak,\tau^*\}>\ell T']\geq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T']
and

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+\frac{3\constrefs{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}{item:taualphamaxup for PP}}{(1-\ctildemaxdown)\npm_{0}}]]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+\frac{\constrefs{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}{item:taualphamaxup for PP}}{\npm_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}}]]

\displaystyle\leq\ell\quantity(\exp(-\Omega(n\delta_0^2))+n\exp\qty(-\Omega\qty(n (\npm_0)^2))).

Note that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\geq(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}/3\geq(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}/3\geq\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
holds.
∎

6.5
Emergence of Unique Strong Opinion

Recall the definition of stopping times in
Definition

5.15
.
We can show the following lemma that assures the emergence and persistence of the unique strong opinion, in a similar manner as in the gossip model.

Lemma 6.10

(Unique strong opinion lemma)
.

Let
x=x(n)
and
y=y(n)
be arbitrary positive functions such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
,
y=\omega(x)
, and
y=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
We have the following:

1.

For some
T=O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
, we have

\Pr\quantity[\tau_{us}^+(y)> T \text{ or } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(8x),\taupsiplus(8x)\}\leq T]\leq O(n^{-10}).

2.

Suppose
\eta_{0}(j)\geq y
for all
j\neq I_{0}
.
Then, for any
T\leq C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:hitting time for tnpm and npm for PP}(\ref{item:tautildemaxdown is large for PP})}}n^{2}
, we have

\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau_{us}^-(y/2),\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T ]\leq n^{-10}.

The proof of
Lemma

6.10
relies on the following lemma.
Essentially, this lemma follows from the drift of
\eta_{t}(j)
in the population protocol model, which is approximately
1/n
times as large as the drift in the gossip model.

Lemma 6.11

(Bounded decrease of
\eta_{t}(j)
)
.

Let
x=x(n)
and
y=y(n)
be arbitrary positive functions such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
,
y=\omega(x)
, and
y=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\eta_{0}(j)\geq y
for all
j\neq I_{0}
.
Then, for any
T\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y/2) \leq T \text{ and } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(x),\taupsiplus(x)\}>T]\leq Tn^{-\omega(1)}.

Weak cannot be strong: Proof of
Lemma

6.11
.

To prove
Lemma

6.11
, we use the following lemma:

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
x=x(n)
,
y=y(n)
, and
I=I(n)
be arbitrary positive functions such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
,
I=\omega(x)
,
y=\omega(I)
, and
y=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose that
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\omega\quantity(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\eta_{0}(j)\in[y,y+I]
.
Then, for any
T\geq\frac{24(1+c_{\eta})}{1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}\frac{In}{(y-I)\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y-I)<\tauetaplus(y+2I) \text{ and } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taumaxup,\taubetaminus(x),\taupsiplus(x)\}>T]\leq n^{-\omega(1)}.

Proof.

Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x)\}
and
let
\tau=\min\{\tau^{+}_{\eta}(y+2I),\tau^{-}_{\eta}(y-I),\tau^{*}\}
.
For
t-1<\tau
, we have
\E_{t-1}[\eta_{t}(j)]\geq\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j)]
and

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j)]

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{t-1}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j)+\frac{\delta_{t-1}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j)}{n}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(I_{t-1})+\alpha_{t-1}(j)+1-2\beta_{t-1})

\displaystyle\geq\eta_{t-1}(j)+\frac{1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}{6(1+c_{\eta})}\cdot\frac{(y-I)\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.11
(
Item

1
) and
Eq.

21
.

Letting
X_{t}=\eta_{t}(j)
and
R=\frac{1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}{6(1+c_{\eta})}\cdot\frac{(y-I)\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\eta_{t-1}(j)+R-\quantity(\eta_{t-1}(j)+\drift-\E_{t-1}[\eta_t(j)])_{t-1}[\eta_{t}(j)]\leq 0
. Further, we have
X_{t-1}+R-X_{t}=\eta_{t-1}(j)+R-\eta_{t}(j)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\eta_{t-1}(j)+\drift-\eta_t(j))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{(\ceta)}(I_{t-1},j)]-\delta_{t}^{(\ceta)}(I_{t-1},j))_{t-1}[\delta_{t}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j)]-\delta_{t}^{(c_{\eta})}(I_{t-1},j),

i.e.,
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(O(1/n),O(\npm_0/n^2))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.11
(
Item

3
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Let
I^{-}=y-I
,
I^{-}_{*}=y
,
I^{+}_{*}=y+I
, and
I^{+}=y+2I
.
Note that
I^{+}-I^{+}_{*}=I^{+}_{*}-I^{-}_{*}=I^{-}_{*}-I^{-}=I
.
Let
T\geq\frac{24(1+c_{\eta})}{1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}\frac{In}{(y-I)\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}\geq\frac{2(I^{+}-X_{0})}{R}
.
Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) for
\eta_{0}(j)\in[I_{*}^{-},I^{+}_{*}]
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y-I)<\tauetaplus(y+2I) \text{ and } \tau^*>T]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(\frac{I^2}{\frac{\npm_0}{n^2}T+\frac{I}{n}}))\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(nI(y-I))).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.11
.

Let
I=I(n)
be a positive parameter such that
I=\omega(x)
and
I=o(y)
.
Let
I^{-}=y-I
,
I^{-}_{*}=y
,
I^{+}_{*}=y+I
, and
I^{+}=y+2I
.
Note that
I^{+}-I^{+}_{*}=I^{+}_{*}-I^{-}_{*}=I^{-}_{*}-I^{-}=I
.
Let
T^{\prime}=\frac{24(1+c_{\eta})}{1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}\frac{In}{(y-I)\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}=o\quantity(\frac{n}{\npm_0})
.
Then, combining

Lemmas

6.1
,
6.2
,
6.6
and
6.5
(
Item

2
),
Lemma

6.12

if
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x\geq 1/2-2x
,
\psi_{0}\leq x\leq 2x
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\omega\quantity(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\eta_{0}(j)\in[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y-I)<\tauetaplus(y+2I)]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y-I)<\tauetaplus(y+2I) \text{ and } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taumaxup,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T']

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taumaxup,\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T']+\Pr\quantity[\taupsiplus(2x)\leq T']

\displaystyle\leq n^{-\omega(1)}.

Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x)\}
.
Let
\tau_{s}^{-}=\inf\{t\geq s:\eta_{t}(j)\leq I^{-}\}
and
\tau_{s}^{+}=\inf\{t\geq s:\eta_{t}(j)\geq I^{+}\}
.
Suppose
\eta_{0}(j)\in[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\psi_{0}\leq x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\geq\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

2
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y-I) \leq T \text{ and } \tau^*>T]\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\eta_s(j)\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*] \text{ and } \tau^*>s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau^-_s<\tau^+_s]]\leq Tn^{-\omega(1)}.

∎

Emergence of unique strong opinion: Proof of
Lemma

6.10
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.10
(
Item

1
).

Fix an arbitrary pair of distinct opinions
i
and
j
.
Observe the following holds:

•

Combining
Items

1
and
2
of
Lemma

6.8
,
for some
T_{1}=O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
,
\min\{\alpha_{T_{1}}(i),\alpha_{T_{1}}(j)\}\leq(1-c_{\mathrm{weak}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}
,
\beta_{T_{1}}\geq 1/2-4x
,
\psi_{T_{1}}\leq 4x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}=\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
with probability at least
1-n^{-10}
.
Suppose that
\alpha_{T_{1}}(j)\leq(1-c_{\mathrm{weak}})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}
without loss of generality.
From the definition of
\eta_{t}(j)
, we have
\eta_{T_{1}}(j)\geq\frac{c_{\mathrm{weak}}-c_{\mathrm{strong}}}{1-c_{\mathrm{strong}}}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}=\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})\geq 2y
.

•

From
Lemma

6.11
, for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{1}+T_{2}]
, where
T_{2}\geq n^{2}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}/C\log n=\Omega(n^{2}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}/\log n)
,
\eta_{t}(j)\geq y
,
\beta_{t}\geq 1/2-8x
,
\psi_{t}\leq 8x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}=\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
with probability at least
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
.

Since
T_{1}=O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})=o(n\sqrt{n})
and
T_{2}=\Omega(n^{2}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}/\log n)=\omega(n\sqrt{n})
, we have
T_{1}<T_{2}
.
Thus, by taking the union bound over all pairs of distinct opinions
i,j\in[k]
, with high probability, there exists
t\leq T_{1}
such that
\eta_{t}(j)\geq y
for all
j\neq I_{t}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.10
(
Item

2
).

Consider an arbitrary opinion
j\neq I_{0}
. Combining
Lemmas

6.11
and
6.7
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tauetaminus(y/2) \leq T \text{ or } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\tauetaminus(y/2) \leq T \text{ or } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T} \text{ and } \min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T]

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tautildemaxdown,\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-11}.

(36)

Thus, from the union bound, we obtain the claim.
∎

6.6
Towards Consensus

Once we have shown that there exists exactly one strong opinion within
O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds, we can show that with high probability, the consensus is reached within
O(n\log n/\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
rounds.
The main lemma in this section is the following:

Lemma 6.13

(Unique strong opinion leads to consensus)
.

Let
x=x(n)
and
y=y(n)
be arbitrary positive functions such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
,
y=\omega(x)
, and
y=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\eta_{0}(j)\geq y
for all
j\neq I_{0}
.
Then,
\Pr\quantity[\taucons>T]\leq 1/n
holds for some
T=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
.

Recall the stopping times defined in
Section

5.7
.
We introduce the following lemmas to show
Lemma

6.13
.
These are analogous to the lemmas in the gossip model, but with different drifts and variances in the Bernstein condition.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
x=x(n)
and
y=y(n)
be arbitrary positive functions such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
,
y=\omega(x)
, and
y=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
We have the following:

1.

Let
c\in(0,1)
be an arbitrary constant.
Suppose
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
,
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
, and
\eta_{0}(j)\geq y
for all
j\neq I_{0}
.
Then, for some
T=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxplus(1-c)>T \text{ or } \min\{\taupsiplus(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T]\leq n^{-10}.

2.

Suppose that
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
, and
\alpha_{0}(1)\geq(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\beta_{0}
.
Then, for some
T=O(n\log n)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxplus(1-4\ctildemaxdown)>T \text{ or } \min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T]\leq n^{-10}.

3.

Suppose that
\alpha_{0}(1)\geq 7/8
. Then,
\Pr\quantity[\tau_{all}> 8n\log n \text{ or } \taumaxminus(3/4)\leq 8n\log n]\leq 1/n.

4.

Suppose
\alpha_{0}(1)=\beta_{0}
and
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
.
Then,
\Pr[\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}>6n\log n]\leq 1/n
.

Proof of
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

1
).

Let
\tau^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}
for some positive constant
c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}\in(0,1)
.
Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau^{+}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x),\tau_{us}^{-}(y/2)\}
and
\tau=\min\{\tau^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau^{*}\}
.
For
t-1<\tau
, in a similar calculation as the proof of
Lemma

5.21
(
Item

1
), we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\tnpm_t]

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\quantity(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\frac{18\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}}{n^{2}}\geq\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}+\frac{c_{\mathrm{strong}}c(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})^{2}}{12n^{2}}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.14
,
Eq.

23

and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}\geq\frac{(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}{3}=\omega(1/n)
.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
and
R=\frac{c_{\mathrm{strong}}c(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})^{2}}{12n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t-1}+R-\quantity(\tnpm_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[\tnpm_t])_{t-1}[\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}]\leq 0

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\tnpm_{t-1}\qty(1+\frac{\npm_{t-1}-\gamma_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2n})-\frac{18\npm_{t-1}}{n^2}-\tnpm_t)

satisfies one-sided
\quantity(O\qty(\frac{1}{n}),O\qty(\frac{\npm_0}{n^2}))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.14
and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) for
I^{+}=(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
,
I^{-}=(1+c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}
, and for
T^{\prime}=\frac{2(I^{+}-X_{0})}{R}=\frac{24}{c_{\mathrm{strong}}c^{+}(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}}\frac{n}{\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxup> T' \text{ and } \tau^* > T']\leq\exp\quantity(- \Omega\qty( n (\npm_0)^2)).

Let
\tau^{\uparrow(s)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq(1+c^{\uparrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{s}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}
.
From definition, for some
\ell=\Theta(\log n)
,
\tau^{+}_{\widetilde{\max}}\leq\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)}
holds.
Applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

3
), we obtain
\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tautildemaxplus,\tau^*\}>\ell T']\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T']
and

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+T']]

\displaystyle\leq\ell\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n (\npm_0)^2)).

Note that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\geq\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
holds.
Finally, combining the above and
Eq.

36
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tautildemaxplus>T \text{ or }\min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\tautildemaxplus>T \text{ or } \min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T} \text{ and } \min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x),\tau_{us}^-(y/2)\}>T]

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taupsi(2x), \taubetaminus(2x),\tau_{us}^-(y/2)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-10}.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

2
).

Let
\tau_{\max}^{\downarrow}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\leq(1-c_{\max}^{\downarrow})\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}
.
Let
\tau^{*}=\min\{\tau_{\max}^{+}(1-4c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}),\tau^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x)\}
and
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\max}^{\uparrow},\tau_{\max}^{\downarrow},\tau^{*}\}
.
For
t-1<\tau
, in a similar calculation as the proof of
Lemma

5.21
(
Item

2
),

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]-\alpha_{t-1}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(1)}{n}\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(1)\qty(\frac{2}{\tnpm_{t-1}}-1))\geq\frac{8(c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}(1-c_{\max}^{\downarrow})}{1-2c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}\frac{\alpha_{0}(1)}{n}.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=\alpha_{t}(1)
and
R=\frac{8(c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}(1-c_{\max}^{\downarrow})}{1-2c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}}\frac{\alpha_{0}(1)}{n}>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\alpha_{t-1}(1)+R-\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(1)+\drift-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(1)])_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]\leq 0

and

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(1)]-\alpha_t(1))_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]-\alpha_{t}(1)\\

satisfies one-sided
\quantity(O\qty(\frac{1}{n}),O\qty(\frac{\alpha_{0}(1)}{n^2}))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.2
(
Item

3
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).

Applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
) for
I^{+}=(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})\alpha_{0}(1)
,
I^{-}=(1-c_{\max}^{\downarrow})\alpha_{0}(1)
, and

T^{\prime}=\frac{c_{\max}^{\uparrow}(1-2c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})}{4(c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})^{2}(1-c_{\max}^{\downarrow})}n=\frac{2(I^{+}-X_{0})}{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxup> T' \text{ and } \tau^* > T']\leq\exp\quantity(- \Omega\qty( n (\npm_0)^2)).

Let
\tau^{\uparrow(s)}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq(1+c_{\max}^{\uparrow})^{s}\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}\}
.
From definition, for some
\ell=\Theta(\log n)
,
\tau_{\max}^{+}(1-4c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\leq\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)}
holds.
Applying
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

3
), we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taumaxplus(1-4\ctildemaxdown),\tau^*\}>\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T']

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+T']]

\displaystyle\leq\ell\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n (\npm_0)^2)).

Note that
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\geq\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})
holds.
Finally, combining the above and
Lemma

6.7
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxplus(1-4\ctildemaxdown)>T \text{ or } \min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\taumaxplus(1-4\ctildemaxdown)>T \text{ or } \min\{\taupsi(2x),\taubetaminus(2x)\}\leq T} \text{ and } \min\{\taupsi(2x), \taubetaminus(2x),\tautildemaxdown\}>T]

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taupsi(2x), \taubetaminus(2x),\tautildemaxdown\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq n^{-10}.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

3
).

To begin with, we prove the following claim.

Claim 6.15
.

Let
c\in(0,1/2)
be an arbitrary constant.
Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose that
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
, and
\alpha_{0}(1)\geq 1-c
.
Then, for some
T=\Omega(n/x)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\taumaxminus(1-2c) \leq\min\{T,\taubetaminus(x),\taupsiplus(x)\}]\leq\exp\quantity(-\Omega\qty(n)).

Proof.

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\beta}^{-}(x),\tau_{\psi}^{+}(x)\}
.
Then, for
t-1<\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t-1}(1)\quantity(1+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(1)-\frac{\gamma_{t-1}+x}{\beta_{t-1}}}{n})\geq\alpha_{t-1}(1)-\frac{3x}{n}.

Note that we use
Lemma

4.2
(
Item

1
) in the first equality.

Hence, letting
X_{t}=\alpha_{t}(1)
and
R=-\frac{3x}{n}<0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\alpha_{t-1}(1)-3x-\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(1)-3x-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(1)])_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_t)_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[\alpha_t(1)]-\alpha_t(1))_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(1)]-\alpha_{t}(1)
satisfies
\quantity(O(1/n),O(1/n))
-Bernstein condition.
Note that we use
Lemma

4.9
(
Item

3
) and
Lemma

3.7
(
Items

2
and
3
).
Hence, applying
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

1
) with
I^{-}=1-2c
, and
T\leq\frac{c}{6x}n\leq\frac{X_{0}-I^{-}}{-2R}
, we obtain the claim.
∎

Write
g_{t}=\beta_{t}-\alpha_{t}(1)=\sum_{j\geq 2}\alpha_{t}(j)
for convenience.
Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{all},\tau^{-}_{\max}(3/4)\}
.
Then, for
t-1<\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[g_{t}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 2}\alpha_{t-1}(j)\quantity(1+\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(j)+1-2\beta_{t-1}}{n})\leq g_{t-1}\quantity(1-\frac{1}{4n}).

Note that
\alpha_{t-1}(j)\leq 1-\alpha_{t-1}(1)\leq 1/4
and
\beta_{t-1}\geq\alpha_{t-1}(1)\geq 3/4
.

Let
r=1-\frac{1}{4n}
,
X_{t}=r^{-t}g_{t}
, and
Y_{t}=X_{t\wedge\tau}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[Y_{t}]-Y_{t-1}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_{t-1})_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t-1}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}r^{-t}\quantity(r^{-t}\E_{t-1}[g_t]-r^{-(t-1)}g_{t-1})_{t-1}[g_{t}]-r^{-(t-1)}g_{t-1}\leq 0,

i.e.,
Y_{t}
is a submartingale.
Hence, we have
\E[Y_{T}]\leq\E[Y_{0}]=g_{0}\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\E[Y_{T}]\geq\E[X_{T}\mid\tau>T]\Pr[\tau>T]=r^{-T}\E[g_{T}\mid\tau>T]\Pr[\tau>T]\geq r^{-T}n^{-1}\Pr[\tau>T].

Consequently, we have

\Pr[\tau>T]\leq nr^{T}\leq n\exp\quantity(-\frac{T}{4n})\leq 1/n^{2}.

Thus, combining the above,
6.15
,
6.1
and
6.2
gives

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau_{all}> T \text{ or } \taumaxminus(3/4)\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\qty{\tau_{all}> T \text{ or } \taumaxminus(3/4)\leq T} \text{ and } \min\{\taumaxminus(3/4),\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T]

\displaystyle+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taumaxminus(3/4),\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\tau>T]+\Pr\quantity[\taumaxminus(3/4)\leq T \text{ and } \min\{\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}>T]+\Pr\quantity[\min\{\taubetaminus(2x),\taupsiplus(2x)\}\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq 1/n.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

4
).

Let
\tau=\min\{\tau_{\mathrm{cons}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}\}
.
Let
u_{t}=1-\alpha_{t}(1)
. Then,

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[u_{t}]=1-\alpha_{t-1}(1)\quantity(1+\frac{1+\alpha_{t-1}(1)-2\beta_{t-1}}{n})=u_{t-1}\quantity(1-\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(1)}{n})\leq u_{t-1}\quantity(1-\frac{1}{3n}).

Let
r=1-\frac{1}{3n}
,
X_{t}=r^{-t}u_{t}
, and
Y_{t}=X_{t\wedge\tau}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[Y_{t}]-Y_{t-1}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[X_t]-X_{t-1})_{t-1}[X_{t}]-X_{t-1}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}r^{-t}\quantity(r^{-t}\E_{t-1}[u_t]-r^{-(t-1)}u_{t-1})_{t-1}[u_{t}]-r^{-(t-1)}u_{t-1}\leq 0,

i.e.,
Y_{t}
is a submartingale.
Hence, we have
\E[Y_{T}]\leq\E[Y_{0}]=u_{0}\leq 1
and

\displaystyle\E[Y_{T}]\geq\E[X_{T}\mid\tau>T]\Pr[\tau>T]=r^{-T}\E[u_{T}\mid\tau>T]\Pr[\tau>T]\geq r^{-T}n^{-1}\Pr[\tau>T].

Consequently, we have

\Pr[\tau>T]\leq nr^{T}\leq n\exp\quantity(-\frac{T}{3n})\leq 1/n^{2}.

Thus, from
Lemma

6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr[\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}>T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr[\min\{\tau_{\mathrm{cons}},\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x)\}>T]+\Pr[\tau_{\beta}^{-}(2x)\leq T]\leq 1/n.

∎

Proof of
Lemma

6.13
.

We have the following:

•

From
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

1
), for some
T_{1}=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
, we have
\alpha_{T_{1}}(1)\geq(1-c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}})\beta_{T_{1}}
,
\beta_{T_{1}}\geq 1/2-2x
, and
\psi_{T_{1}}\leq 2x
, with probability at least
1-n^{-10}
.

•

From
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

2
), for some
T_{2}=O(n\log n)
, we have
\alpha_{T_{1}+T_{2}}(1)\geq 1-4c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}
,
\beta_{T_{1}+T_{2}}\geq 1/2-4x
, and
\psi_{T_{1}+T_{2}}\leq 4x
, with probability at least
1-n^{-10}
.

•

Assume that
c^{\downarrow}_{\widetilde{\max}}=1/32
. From
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

3
), for some
T_{3}=O(n\log n)
, we have
\alpha_{T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}}(1)=\beta_{T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}}
and
\alpha_{T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}}(1)\geq 3/4
with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
.

•

From
Lemma

6.14
(
Item

4
), for some
T_{4}=O(n\log n)
, we have
\alpha_{T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}}(1)=1
with probability at least
1-1/n^{2}
.

Thus, we obtain the claim.
∎

6.7
Putting All Together

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
C>0
be any constant.
Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
For some

\displaystyle T=\begin{cases}O\quantity(n\log n)&\quantity(\text{if } k\le\frac{C\sqrt{n}}{(\log n)^2}),\\
O\quantity(n^{1.5}(\log n)^3)&(\text{otherwise})\end{cases},

(37)

we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\psi_T\leq x \text{ and } \beta_T\geq\frac{1}{2}-x \text{ and } \npm_T\geq\frac{(\log n)^{1.5}}{\sqrt{n}}]\geq 1-O\quantity(n^{-10}).

Proof.

First, from
Lemmas

6.1
and
6.2
, we have the following:

1.

Combining
Items

1
,
2
and
3
of
Lemma

6.1
, for some
T_{1}=O(n\log n)
and
T_{2}=\Omega(n^{3})
,
\min_{t\in[T_{2}]}\beta_{T_{1}+t}\geq 1/2-x/2
holds with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
.

2.

Combining
Items

1
and
2
of
Lemma

6.2
, for some
T_{1}^{\prime}=O(n\log n)
and
T_{2}^{\prime}=\Omega(n^{3})
,
\min_{t\in[T_{2}^{\prime}]}\psi_{T_{1}^{\prime}+t}\leq x/2
holds with probability at least
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
.

Items

1
and
2
implies that, for some
T=O(n\log n)
,
\beta_{T}\geq 1/2-x/2
and
\psi_{T}\leq x/2
hold with probability at least
1-O(n^{-10})
.
For the case where
k=O\quantity(\sqrt{n}/(\log n)^2)
,
since
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}\geq\beta_{t}/k=\Omega(\beta_{t}(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n})
holds for any
t
, we obtain the claim.
For the general case, from
Lemma

6.3
, for some
T^{\prime}=O\quantity(n^{1.5}(\log n)^3)
, we have that
\gamma_{T+T^{\prime}}\geq(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n}
,
\psi_{T+T^{\prime}}\leq x
, and
\beta_{T+T^{\prime}}\geq 1/2-x
with probability at least
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
.

∎

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
x=x(n)
be an arbitrary positive function such that
x=\omega(\sqrt{\log n/n})
and
x=o(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Suppose that
\psi_{0}\leq x
,
\beta_{0}\geq 1/2-x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0}=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
.
Then,
\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
with high probability.

Proof.

We have the following:

•

From
Lemma

6.10
(
Item

1
), for some
T_{1}=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
, we have
\min_{j\neq I_{T_{1}}}\eta_{T_{1}}(j)\geq y
,
\beta_{T_{1}}\geq 1/2-8x
,
\psi_{T_{1}}\leq 8x
, and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T_{1}}=\Omega(\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{0})=\omega(\log n/\sqrt{n})
with probability at least
1-n^{-10}
.

•

From
Lemma

6.13
, for some
T_{2}=O\quantity(n\log n/\npm_0)
, we have
\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}\leq T_{1}+T_{2}
with probability at least
1-1/n
.

Thus, we obtain the claim.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

From
Lemma

6.16
, we have that
\psi_{T}\leq x
and
\beta_{T}\geq 1/2-x
and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T}\geq(\log n)^{2}/\sqrt{n}
hold with high probability for some
T
as defined in
Eq.

37
(
Lemma

6.16
).
Then, from
Lemma

5.24
, we reach a consensus within additional
O\quantity(\frac{n\log n}{\npm_T})=O\quantity( \min\qty{ kn\log n, n^{1.5}/\sqrt{\log n} } )
steps with high probability.
Here, we use
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T}\geq\frac{\beta_{T}}{k}=\Omega(k)
if
k
is small and
\alpha^{\mathrm{max}}_{T}\geq(\log n)^{1.5}/\sqrt{n}
if
k
is large.
Therefore, the consensus time is bounded by

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{cons}}\leq T+O\quantity( \min\qty{ kn\log n, n^{1.5}/\sqrt{\log n}} )=\widetilde{O}(\min\quantity{kn, n^{1.5}})

and obtain the claim.
∎

Appendix A
Technical Inequalities and Auxiliary Lemmas

This appendix collects several technical tools that are used repeatedly in the analysis but are not specific to USD.
We include variants of Chernoff-type bounds, concentration results for read-
\ell
families, the optional stopping theorem, and basic notions such as stochastic domination.
None of these results are new; they are provided here for completeness and to keep the main proofs self-contained.

Lemma A.1

(
[
16
, Corollary 1.10.4]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables that take values in
[0,1]
.
Let
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
.
Then, for any
h\geq 2\mathrm{e}\E[X]
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[X \ge h]\leq 2^{-h}.

While the Chernoff bound gives a tail bound for the sum of independent random variables, we will need a more general concentration result for the sum of random variables that are not necessarily independent.
In the following, we introduce the notion of
read-
\ell
family
, which is a family of random variables that are not necessarily independent but have a certain structure
[
24
,
18
]
.

Definition A.2

(Read-
\ell
family;
[
24
]
)
.

A family of real-valued random variables
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
is called a
read-
\ell
family
if there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}
, independent random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
, and subsets
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}\subseteq[m]
that satisfy the following:

1.

Each
Y_{i}
can be written as a function of
(x_{s})_{s\in S_{i}}
for
i\in[n]
.

2.

For each
j\in[m]
, the number of subsets
S_{i}
that contain
j
is at most
\ell
.

[
24
]
shows for the first time a tail bound for the sum of a read-
\ell
family using an information-theoretic argument, which does not yield a bound for the moment generating function.
Very recently,
[
18
]
shows a concentration result for the sum of a read-
\ell
family based on the moment generating function using the following general inequality.

Lemma A.3

(
[
18
, Lemma 16]
)
.

Let
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
be a read-
\ell
family.
Then,
\E\quantity[\prod_{i\in[n]}F_i]\leq\prod_{i\in[n]}\quantity(\prod_{i\in[n]}\E[F_i^\ell])[F_{i}^{\ell}]^{1/\ell}
.

Note that, if
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
forms a read-
1
family, then so does the family
(F_{1},\dots,F_{n})
for
F_{i}=\exp\quantity(\lambda Y_i)
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
Applying
Lemma

A.3
to this family
(F_{1},\dots,F_{n})
yields a bound for the moment generating function.

Theorem A.4

(Optional stopping theorem. See, e.g., Theorem 4.8.5 of
[
19
]
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a submartingale (resp. supermartingale) such that
\E_{t-1}\left[|X_{t}-X_{t-1}|\right]<\infty
a.s. and let
\tau
be a stopping time such that
\E[\tau]<\infty
.
Then,
\E[X_{\tau}]\geq\E[X_{0}]
(resp.
\E[X_{\tau}]\leq\E[X_{0}]
).

Definition A.5

(Stochastic domination)
.

For two random variables
X
and
Y
, we say that
Y
stochastically dominates
X
, written as
X\preceq Y
, if for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\Pr\quantity[X\leq\lambda]\geq\Pr\quantity[Y\leq\lambda]
.

Lemma A.6

(
[
31
, Lemma 5.1]
)
.

Let
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a Markov chain over a state space
\Omega
associated with natural filtration
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and let
\tau
be any stopping time with respect to
\mathcal{F}
.
Let
\varphi\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function.
For a parameter
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, let
\tau^{+}_{\varphi}(x)=\inf\quantity{t\ge 0 \colon\varphi(Z_t) \ge x}
.
Let
T,x_{0},c_{\varphi}^{\uparrow}>0,x^{*}>x_{0}
be parameters and
\Omega^{*}\subseteq\Omega
be the set of states
z\in\Omega
such that
\varphi(z)\leq x^{*}
.
Suppose that the following holds:

(i)

There exists
C_{1}>0
such that for any
z\in\Omega^{*}
,

\Pr\quantity[\min\qty{\tauphiplus(x_0), \tau}\le T\condition Z_0=z]\geq C_{1}.

(ii)

Define
\tau^{\uparrow}_{\varphi}=\inf\quantity{ t\ge 0 \colon\varphi(Z_t) \ge(1+\cphiup) \cdot\varphi(Z_0) }
. Then, there exists
C_{2}>0
such that for any
z\in\Omega^{*}
,

\Pr\quantity[ \min\qty{\tauphiup, \tau} \le T \condition Z_0 = z ]\geq 1-\exp\quantity( - C_2 \varphi(z)^2 ).

Then, there exists a constant
C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:nazo lemma}}}=C_{\mbox{\tiny\ref{lem:nazo lemma}}}(C_{1},C_{2},c_{\varphi}^{\uparrow},x_{0})>0
(independent of
x^{*}
) such that,
for any
z\in\Omega^{*}
and any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[ \min\qty{\tauphiplus(x^*), \tau} \le\constref{lem:nazo lemma}\cdot T\cdot\qty(\log(1/\varepsilon) + \log(x^*/x_0)) \condition Z_0 = z]\geq 1-\varepsilon.

A.1
Drift Analysis Results

In this section, we present some drift analysis tools that are useful in our analysis for USD.

Proof of
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

1
).

We apply
Lemma

3.9
(
Item

1
) to
Y_{t}=-X_{t}
with
R_{Y}=-R>0
.
We have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[Y_t]-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)_{t-1}[Y_{t}]-Y_{t-1}-R_{Y}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(Y_t-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition.
Hence, letting
h=X_{0}-I^{-}
,
T=\frac{(1-\varepsilon)h}{-R}=\frac{(1-\varepsilon)h}{R_{Y}}
, and
z=h-R_{Y}T=\varepsilon h=\varepsilon(X_{0}-I^{-})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^- \le\min\{T,\tau\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{\epsilon^2(X_0-I^-)^2/2}{\variance T + (\epsilon(X_0-I^-) \bounded)/3}).

Note that
\tau^{-}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq I^{-}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}\geq Y_{0}+h\}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.11
(
Item

2
).

We have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>T \text{ and } \tau^*>T]

\displaystyle=\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>T \text{ and } \tau^*>T \text{ and } \tau^->T]+\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>T \text{ and } \tau^*>T \text{ and } \tau^-\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^+,\tau^-,\tau^*\}>T]+\Pr\quantity[\tau^-\leq\min\{T,\tau^+,\tau^*\}]

and

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>\tau^- \text{ and } \tau^*>T]

\displaystyle=\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>\tau^- \text{ and } \tau^*>T \text{ and } \tau^->T]+\Pr\quantity[\tau^+>\tau^- \text{ and } \tau^*>T \text{ and } \tau^-\leq T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^+,\tau^-,\tau^*\}>T]+\Pr\quantity[\tau^-\leq\min\{T,\tau^+,\tau^*\}].

For
\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^+,\tau^-,\tau^*\}>T]
, we apply
Lemma

3.9
(
Item

2
) to
Y_{t}=-X_{t}
with
R_{Y}=-R<0
.
We have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[Y_t]-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)_{t-1}[Y_{t}]-Y_{t-1}-R_{Y}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(Y_t-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition.
Hence, letting
h=I^{+}-X_{0}
,

T=\frac{(1+\varepsilon)h}{R}=\frac{(1+\varepsilon)h}{-R_{Y}}
, and
z=(-R_{Y})T-h=\varepsilon h=\varepsilon(I^{+}-X_{0})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^+,\tau\} >T]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{\epsilon^2(I^+-X_0)^2/2}{\variance T + (\epsilon(I^+-X_0) \bounded)/3}).

Note that
\tau^{+}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq I^{+}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}\leq Y_{0}-h\}
.

For
\Pr\quantity[\tau^-\leq\min\{T,\tau^+,\tau^*\}]
, we apply
Lemma

3.9
(
Item

1
) to
Y_{t}=-X_{t}
and
R_{Y}=0
.
Then, since
R>0
, we have

\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(\E_{t-1}[Y_t]-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)_{t-1}[Y_{t}]-Y_{t-1}-R_{Y}\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}X_{t-1}+R-\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-\E_{t-1}[X_t])_{t-1}[X_{t}]\leq 0

and
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(Y_t-Y_{t-1}-\drift_Y)\leq\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t-1}\quantity(X_{t-1}+\drift-X_t)
satisfies one-sided
\quantity(\bounded,\variance)
-Bernstein condition.
Hence, letting
z=h=X_{0}-I^{-}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^- \le\min\{T,\tau\}]\leq\exp\quantity(- \frac{(X_0-I^-)^2/2}{\variance T + ((X_0-I^-) \bounded)/3}).

Note that
\tau^{-}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\leq I^{-}\}=\inf\{t\geq 0:Y_{t}\geq Y_{0}+h\}
.

∎

Lemma A.7
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a sequence of random variables and
let
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be a filtration such that
X_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
t\geq 0
.
Let
\tau^{*}
be a stopping time with respect to
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
We have the following:

1.

Let
c^{\uparrow},c^{\downarrow}>0
be positive constants.
For
s\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\tau^{\uparrow}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq s:X_{t}\geq(1+c^{\uparrow})X_{s}\},\;\tau^{\downarrow}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq s:X_{t}\leq(1-c^{\downarrow})X_{s}\}.

Suppose
X_{0}>0
.
Then, for
\tau^{\downarrow}=\tau_{0}^{\downarrow}
and any
T>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^\downarrow\leq T \text{ and } \tau^*> T]\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{X_s\geq X_0 \text{ and } \tau^*> s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau_s^\downarrow\leq\min\{T,\tau_s^\uparrow,\tau^*\}]].

2.

Let
I^{-}<I^{-}_{*}<I^{+}_{*}<I^{+}
be parameters.
For
s\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\tau^{+}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq s\colon X_t\geq I^+},\;\tau^{-}_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t\geq s\colon X_t\leq I^-}.

Let
\tau^{jump}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\quantity{t> 0: X_t\leq X_{t-1} - (I^{+}_*-I^-_*)}
.
Then, for
\tau^{-}=\tau_{0}^{-}
,
X_{0}\geq I^{-}_{*}
and
T>0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^-\leq T \text{ and } \min\{\tau^{jump},\tau^*\}> T]\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{X_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*] \text{ and } \tau^*>s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau^-_s<\tau^+_s]].

3.

Let
c^{\uparrow}>0
be a positive constant.
For
s\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\tau^{\uparrow(s)}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf\{t\geq 0:X_{t}\geq(1+c^{\uparrow})^{s}X_{0}\}.

Suppose
X_{0}>0
.
Then, for any
T>0
and
\ell>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T]\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+T]].

Proof of
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

1
).

First, we claim the following:
Let
\omega
be any sample path such that
\tau^{\downarrow}(\omega)\leq T
.
Then, there exists
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
such that
X_{s}(\omega)\geq X_{0}(\omega)
and
\tau_{s}^{\downarrow}(\omega)\leq\min\{T,\tau_{s}^{\uparrow}(\omega)\}
hold.

To see this, define
s
to be the largest integer such that
X_{s}(\omega)=\max_{0\leq t<\tau^{\downarrow}(\omega)}X_{t}(\omega)
holds.
For such
s
, we have
X_{s}(\omega)\geq X_{0}(\omega)
and
\tau_{s}^{\downarrow}(\omega)<\tau_{s}^{\uparrow}(\omega)
.
Furthermore,
\tau_{s}^{\downarrow}(\omega)\leq\tau^{\downarrow}(\omega)\leq T
since
(1-c^{\downarrow})X_{s}(\omega)\geq(1-c^{\downarrow})X_{0}(\omega)
.
Thus,
\tau_{s}^{\downarrow}(\omega)\leq\min\{T,\tau_{s}^{\uparrow}(\omega)\}
holds and this completes the proof of the claim.

From the above claim, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^\downarrow\leq T \text{ and } \tau^*> T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\exists s\in\{0,\ldots,T-1\}: X_s\geq X_0 \text{ and } \tau_s^\downarrow\leq\min\{T,\tau_s^\uparrow\} \text{ and } \tau^*> T]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\Pr\quantity[X_s\geq X_0 \text{ and } \tau_s^\downarrow\leq\min\{T,\tau_s^\uparrow\} \text{ and } \tau^*> T \text{ and } \tau^*> s]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{X_s\geq X_0 \text{ and } \tau^*> s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau_s^\downarrow\leq\min\{T,\tau_s^\uparrow,\tau^*\}]].

∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

2
).

First, we claim the following:
Let
\omega
be any sample path such that
\tau^{-}(\omega)\leq T
and
\tau^{jump}(\omega)>T
.
Then, there exists
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
such that
I^{-}_{*}\leq X_{s}(\omega)\leq I^{+}_{*}
and
\tau^{-}_{s}(\omega)<\tau^{+}_{s}(\omega)
.

To see this, define
s\in\{0,1,\ldots,T-1\}
to be the largest integer such that

X_{s}(\omega)\in[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
.
Such an
s
exists since
X_{0}(\omega)\geq I^{-}_{*}
and
\tau^{jump}(\omega)>T
.
By the definition of
s
, we have
X_{t}(\omega)\notin[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
for any
t>s
.
Furthermore, since
\tau^{jump}(\omega)>T
, that is, the process never jumps
across the interval
[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
in one step, it follows that
X_{t}(\omega)\leq I^{+}_{*}
for
all
t\geq s
, and hence
\tau^{-}_{s}(\omega)<\tau^{+}_{s}(\omega)
.
Indeed, if there existed some
t\geq s
such that
X_{t}(\omega)>I^{+}_{*}
, then the occurrence
of
\tau^{-}_{s}(\omega)<\tau^{+}_{s}(\omega)
would imply the existence of a time
t^{\prime}>t
for which

X_{t^{\prime}}(\omega)\in[I^{-}_{*},I^{+}_{*}]
, contradicting the definition of
s
.

Thus,

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\tau^-\leq T \text{ and } \tau^{jump}>T \text{ and } \tau^*> T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\exists s \in\{0,1,\ldots, T-1\}: \tau^{-}_s<\tau^{+}_s \text{ and } X_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*] \text{ and } \tau^*> T]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\Pr\quantity[X_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*] \text{ and } \tau^{-}_s<\tau^{+}_s \text{ and } \tau^*> s]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=0}^{T-1}\E\quantity[\indicator_{X_s\in[I^-_*,I^{+}_*] \text{ and } \tau^*> s}\Pr_s\qty[\tau^{-}_s<\tau^{+}_s]].

∎

Proof of
Lemma

A.7
(
Item

3
).

Let
\sigma_{0}=0
and for
s\geq 1
, let

\displaystyle\sigma_{s}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^{*},\sigma_{s-1}+T\}.

First, we claim the following:
If the event
\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^{*}\}>\ell T
occurs, then there exists
s\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
such that the events
\sigma_{s-1}=\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}
,
\tau^{*}>\sigma_{s-1}
, and
\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^{*}\}>\sigma_{s-1}+T
occur.
To see this, define
s
to be the smallest integer such that
\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^{*}\}>\sigma_{s-1}+T
.
By the minimality of
s
, for any
i\leq s-1
, we have
\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(i)},\tau^{*}\}\leq\sigma_{i-1}+T
.
Moreover,
\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^{*}\}>\sigma_{s-1}+T
implies
\tau^{*}>\sigma_{s-1}+T
, and hence
\tau^{*}>\sigma_{s-1}
.
Thus, for any
i\leq s-1
, it follows that
\tau^{*}>\sigma_{i-1}
, and therefore
\sigma_{i}=\tau^{\uparrow(i)}
.
This proves the claim.
From the claim, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr\quantity[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(\ell)},\tau^*\}>\ell T]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\quantity[\exists s \in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}: \sigma_{s-1}=\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)} \text{ and } \tau^*>\sigma_{s-1} \text{ and } \min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \sigma_{s-1}+T]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\Pr\quantity[\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)} \text{ and } \min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+T]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{\ell}\E\quantity[\indicator_{\tau^*>\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}} \Pr_{\tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}}\qty[\min\{\tau^{\uparrow(s)},\tau^*\}> \tau^{\uparrow(s-1)}+T]].

∎

Appendix B
Proof of Basic Properties

In this section, we give a proof for basic inequalities for the key quantities in the gossip and population protocol models.
We first introduce the following lemma that will be used in the proof of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\mathrm{max}}_{t}
.

Lemma B.1
.

Define a function
f(x,y)
as

\displaystyle f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\frac{x}{y}&\text{if }y>0,\\
0&\text{if }y=0.\end{cases}

Then, for non-negative random variables
X
and
Y
such that
0\leq X\leq Y
a.s. and
\E[Y]>0
, we have the following:

1.

\E\quantity[f(X,Y)]\geq\frac{\E[X]}{\E[Y]}-\frac{\Cov[X,Y]}{\E[Y]^{2}}
.

2.

\frac{\E[X]}{\E[Y]}-f(X,Y)-\frac{\Cov[X,Y]}{\E[Y]^{2}}\leq\frac{2\quantity(\E[X]-X)[X]-X}{\E[Y]}+\frac{XY-\E[XY]}{\E[Y]^{2}}
a.s.

Proof.

Since
Item

1
follows immediately by taking expectations on both sides of
Item

2
, we give a proof of
Item

2
.

First, we observe that the following inequality holds: For any
a\geq 0
,
b>0
,
x\geq 0
, and
y\geq x\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{a}{b}-f(x,y)\leq\frac{a-x}{b}+\frac{x}{b^{2}}(y-b).

To see this, consider the following two cases:
y=0
and
y>0
.
If
y=0
, then
f(x,y)=0
and
x=0
from the definition.
Thus,
\frac{a}{b}-f(x,y)=\frac{a}{b}=\frac{a-x}{b}+\frac{x}{b^{2}}(y-b)
and the inequality holds.
If
y>0
, then the inequality holds since
\frac{a}{b}-f(x,y)=\frac{a}{b}-\frac{x}{y}=\frac{a-x}{b}+\frac{x}{b^{2}}(y-b)-\frac{x\quantity(y-b)^{2}}{b^{2}y}
.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\E[X]}{\E[Y]}-f(X,Y)-\frac{\Cov[X,Y]}{\E[Y]^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\E[X]-X}{\E[Y]}+\frac{X}{\E[Y]^{2}}(Y-\E[Y])-\frac{\Cov[X,Y]}{\E[Y]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\quantity(\E[X]-X)[X]-X}{\E[Y]}+\frac{XY-\E[XY]}{\E[Y]^{2}}.

∎

B.1
Gossip Model

By definition of the gossip USD (Definition
3.2
), we have for any
v\in V
and
t\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\Pr_{t-1}[\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i]=\begin{cases}\alpha_{t-1}(i)+1-\beta_{t-1}&(\text{if }\mathrm{opn}_{t-1}(v)=i),\\
\alpha_{t-1}(i)&(\text{if }\mathrm{opn}_{t-1}(v)=\bot),\\
0&(\text{otherwise}).\end{cases}

(38)

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

(i) Expectation.

From
Eq.

38
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V:\mathrm{opn}_{t-1}(v)=i}\Pr_{t-1}[\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i]+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{v\in V:\mathrm{opn}_{t-1}(v)=\bot}\Pr_{t-1}[\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i]

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+1-\beta_{t-1})+(1-\beta_{t-1})\alpha_{t-1}(i)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+2(1-\beta_{t-1})).

(ii) Variance.

Since
\alpha_{t}(i)=(\sum_{v\in V}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i})/n
, then from
Eq.

38
,

\displaystyle\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v\in V}\Var_{t-1}\quantity[\indicator_{\opn_t(v)=i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v\in V}\Pr_{t-1}[\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i]\Pr_{t-1}[\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)\neq i]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}\quantity(\alpha_{t-1}(i)+1-\beta_{t-1})\quantity(\beta_{t-1}-\alpha_{t-1}(i))+\frac{1-\beta_{t-1}}{n}\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(1-\alpha_{t-1}(i))

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}\quantity[
(1-\beta_{t-1})(1+\beta_{t-1}-2\alpha_{t-1}(i))
+\alpha_{t-1}(i)(\beta_{t-1}-\alpha_{t-1}(i)) ].

Thus,

\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\leq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}

and

\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\geq\frac{(1-\beta_{t-1})^{2}\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}.

(iii) Bernstein condition.

For
v\in V
, define

X_{t}(v)=\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i}-\quantity(\indicator_{\opn_t(v)=i}
-\E_{t-1}[\indicator_{\opn_t(v)=i}])_{t-1}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i}].

Since
\lvert X_{t}(v)\rvert\leq 1/n
, Lemma
3.7
(i)
implies that
X_{t}(v)
satisfies a

\tfrac{1}{n},\,\quantity(\tfrac{1}{n},\,\Var_{t-1}[X_t(v)])_{t-1}[X_{t}(v)]\text{-Bernstein condition}.

Because

\alpha_{t}(i)-\E_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]=\sum_{v\in V}X_{t}(v)

and the variables
X_{t}(v)
,
v\in V
are independent, Lemma
3.7
(v)
implies that this sum satisfies a

\tfrac{1}{n},\,\sum_{v\in V}\quantity(\tfrac{1}{n},\,\sum_{v\in V}\Var_{t-1}[X_t(v)])_{t-1}[X_{t}(v)]\text{-Bernstein condition}.

Using
Item

2
of Lemma
4.2
,

\sum_{v\in V}\Var_{t-1}[X_{t}(v)]=\Var_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i)]\leq\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}.

Thus the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.3
.

(i) Expectation.

From
Item

1
of
Lemma

4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\E_{t-1}\quantity[\npo_t]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[k]}\alpha_{t-1}(i)\quantity(\np_{t-1}(i)+2\qty(1-\npo_{t-1}))=\gamma_{t-1}+2\beta_{t-1}(1-\beta_{t-1}).

(ii) Variance.

We begin by calculating
\mathbf{Cov}_{t-1}\quantity[\alpha_t(i),\alpha_t(j)]
and
\mathbf{Cov}_{t-1}\quantity[\alpha_t(i),\beta_t]
, which will be used later.

Claim B.2
.

For any distinct
i,j\in[k]
and
t\geq 1
,

\Cov_{t-1}[\alpha_{t}(i),\alpha_{t}(j)]=-\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)\alpha_{t-1}(j)}{n}\quantity(1-\beta_{t-1})
.

Proof.

From
Eq.

38
,

\displaystyle\Cov_{t-1}\quantity[\alpha_t(i), \alpha_t(j)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v\in V}\sum_{u\in V}\Cov_{t-1}\quantity[\indicator_{\opn_{t}(v)=i},
\indicator_{\opn_{t}(u)=j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v\in V}\Cov_{t-1}\quantity[\indicator_{\opn_t(v)=i},
\indicator_{\opn_t(v)=j}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{v\in V}\E_{t-1}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=i}]\E_{t-1}[\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{opn}_{t}(v)=j}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-\beta_{t-1}}{n}\alpha_{t-1}(i)\alpha_{t-1}(j).

∎

Claim B.3
.

For any
i\in[k]
and
t\geq 1
, it holds that

\mathbf{Cov}_{t-1}\quantity[\alpha_t(i),\beta_t]=\frac{\alpha_{t-1}(i)}{n}\quantity[(1-\beta_{t-1})(1-\alpha_{t-1}(i))+\alpha_{t-1}(i)(\beta_{t-1}-\alpha_{t-1}(i))].

Specifically,
